title: Release Notes - Guard Core
description: Release notes for Guard Core, detailing new features, improvements, and bug fixes

---

Release Notes
=============

___

Unreleased
----------

### Added

- **`get_cache_stats()` reports the hit rate of every detection cache, and `get_performance_stats()` carries it under `caches` (LEDGER 6.484).** `SusPatternsManager.get_cache_stats()` returns hits, misses, evictions, size, maximum size and `hit_rate` (`None` before the first lookup) for the `PatternCompiler` cache of compiled patterns (`compiled_patterns`), the linear-time path programs (`linear_path_patterns`) and, when `detection_pattern_validation_cache_path` is set, the validation verdict cache (`pattern_validation`). The counters start at first use and survive `clear_cache()`. Measured with `python -m perf_gate.cache_hit_rate`: 20 custom patterns added at two boots on one cache file hit 0 of 20 at the first boot and 20 of 20 at the second. The compiled-pattern cache reports 20 misses and 20 hits for those 20 adds (hit rate 0.5: each add compiles once for the safety check and reuses that compile for storage, and the cache is cleared after every add). The blocked user-agent patterns and `add_pattern` compile through that cache (a pattern added is compiled once for the safety check and once for storage, and a blocked user-agent pattern is compiled once per pattern set, not once per request), so its hit rate rises with traffic; the built-in rows are compiled when the pattern set is built and are not looked up again. The performance-tuning checklist of the adapters lists the pattern validation hit rate again.

- **A secret or an email in a flagged URL path stays out of the log line and the event preview (LEDGER 6.316), and seven more encodings are decoded before the scan (LEDGER 6.315).** A path segment that is an email address or a long random-looking token (20 or more letters, digits and `_-.~+=` with at least four class changes, lower to upper or letter to digit) is rendered `[REDACTED]` in the detection log line, the `pattern_detected` preview and every other URL display; the text that matched the attack stays visible and the message and fields are unchanged. The decoders now read an HTML hex entity without `;` (`&#x3cscript&#x3ealert`), octal escapes (`\074`), UTF-7 shifted runs (`+ADw-`), a hex run that spells printable ASCII text (hashes, ids and binary-looking values stay as they are), zlib inside base64, a multipart part with `Content-Transfer-Encoding: quoted-printable` and a raw gzip request body. A compressed payload (a gzip body, gzip or zlib inside a base64 value) is judged like its unpacked content sent plain, under `fail_secure` and fail-open alike: it unpacks into at most `detection_max_body_inspect_bytes` (that cap alone bounds memory and time), the unpacked prefix is scanned like a plain value or body of that size, and unpacked content past the cap takes the outcome a plain body past the cap takes (a `failed` body read when a `SecurityConfig` sets the cap). A cut alone is never a pattern threat and never a `decode_budget_exhausted`. A wire size under the cap no longer means the content is under it: a route that takes large gzip bodies raises `detection_max_body_inspect_bytes` (per route through `max_request_size`), as it does for large plain bodies. The refusal detail says the unpacked content is past the cap, and adds that it is a likely decompression bomb when it is more than 100 times the compressed size and more than 16384 bytes; that wording never changes a verdict.
- **`endpoint_rate_limit_key_problems(endpoint_rate_limits)` returns the engine's own rejection lines for the routing-anomalous keys of an `endpoint_rate_limits` mapping.** It takes a mapping of endpoint to `(requests, window)` and returns one line per key with a dot segment, an empty segment, a backslash or an encoded dot or separator (in any decoding round), in mapping order and with exactly the text `find_malformed_values` reports for the same key, so a caller (the SaaS rule editor, a config linter) can refuse a bad key with the lines the engine would give; an empty list means no key is refused. It is exported from `guard_core` and `guard_core.sync` and is what the engine itself calls, so the text cannot fork. The config-time message for the same keys is unchanged.
- **A synchronous `geo_ip_handler` works in the async engine (LEDGER 6.252, 6.258).** The async engine keeps every handler it was given, as v4.4.0 did, and now uses the plain return value of `initialize`, `initialize_redis`, `initialize_agent` and `ensure_database_loaded` as is while it still awaits an awaitable, so a synchronous handler blocks a country end to end in the async engine. The change adds no call to a lookup of an initialized handler, including the built-in `IPInfoManager`.
- **`apply_security_headers_settings(manager, config)` applies the `security_headers` settings to a `SecurityHeadersManager`.** It does what each adapter's `_configure_security_headers` did (the same keys, defaults, `enabled` handling and CORS fields), so an adapter can call it in place of its own copy. It is exported next to `security_headers_manager` from `guard_core` and `guard_core.sync`. `SecurityConfig.security_headers` is typed `dict[str, object] | None` and accepts and refuses the same values as before.
- **`ipinfo_download_url` (`SecurityConfig`).** The GeoIP database download URL is configurable, https only (`http://` raises a validation error), and defaults to today's `https://ipinfo.io/data/free/country_asn.mmdb`, so nothing changes by default and the cached file at `ipinfo_db_path` is reused. `https://ipinfo.io/data/ipinfo_lite.mmdb` is IPinfo's current file; IPinfo labels the default "Legacy". The Bearer token goes only to the configured host (a cross-origin redirect drops it, in both the async and the sync tree).
- **A bounded `.body` for `custom_response_modifier`** (`custom_response_modifier_max_body_bytes`, default `262144`, range 1024-10485760). `response.body` inside the modifier works when the response declares a `Content-Length` up to the cap and is not `text/event-stream`, HEAD, 204 or 304; otherwise it raises `guard_core.exceptions.ResponseBodyUnavailableError` (an `AttributeError` subclass), one sampled WARNING and one `response_body_unavailable` event go out, and header edits still apply. No modifier and a header-only modifier get one call and no read-ahead, as before. A modifier that reads `.body` is called a second time with the bytes (the first call's changes are discarded, a replay never replays), so code before the first `.body` access can run twice; mark a modifier with side effects with `guard_core.core.responses.reads_response_body` to call it once with the body already read. The bounded read happens once per response and is shared with a `return_pattern` scan; sync engines read the prefix on the `.body` access inside one call. Adapters must implement `read_body_prefix(max_bytes)` so it returns the whole body when `max_bytes` covers its declared length. Before this, `.body` raised inside the modifier on bypassed routes and streamed responses and the response went out unmodified.
- **Events name the rule that decided the request, and the two detection events share an id.** An event carries `matched_rule_type="dynamic"` and `matched_rule_id` (the dynamic rule set's `rule_id`) when an entry of the applied set decided it: a ban the rule owns, a country, cloud provider or user-agent pattern in its lists, its global or endpoint rate limit, its emergency mode, or a detection pattern whose source text it lists. When the same entry is also in the static configuration or built in, the event also carries `matched_rule_overlaps_static: true`. Toggles, thresholds, allow decisions and a set that is not applied never attribute. The four behavioral paths that did not yet carry `matched_rule_type="behavioral"` and the rule's uuid5 now do. `penetration_attempt` carries `threat_categories` and `category` in active and passive mode, `pattern_detected` carries the request `endpoint`, and both carry the same random per-request `correlation_id` (no address or user data in it). The conformance corpus is regenerated.
- **`redis_pool_timeout`, `rate_limit_max_tracked_keys` and `ban_counters_max_tracked_ips`** (`SecurityConfig`). `redis_pool_timeout` (default `1.0`, `> 0`) is how long a Redis call waits for a free pooled connection; the other two (default `10000`, `>= 1`) cap the in-memory rate-limit windows and the auto-ban violation counters one process keeps, so nothing changes by default.
- **`ip_ban_refused` event.** A ban the self-DoS guard refuses (loopback, a trusted proxy) is reported once per IP per ban window, with `duration`, `refusal` and `ban_reason` in the metadata. guard-agent and the SaaS must accept the new event type.
- **`enable_recidive`, `recidive_factor`, `recidive_window` and `recidive_max_duration`** (`SecurityConfig`, off by default). A client banned again inside `recidive_window` (24 h from the end of its last ban) is banned for `recidive_factor ** k` times the duration (k earlier bans), capped at `recidive_max_duration` (7 days) and never below the duration the ban asked for. Threshold bans and behavioral rule bans count and escalate; operator CIDR bans, dynamic-rule bans and manual `ban_ip` calls do not. The history is `{redis_prefix}recidive:<identity>` in Redis, a new key family that ports and the SaaS never read; the `ip_banned` event gains `recidive_count` in its metadata when it escalates.
- **A value that is only `UNION SELECT` is flagged again, with `flag_bare_union_select` (`SecurityConfig`, default `True`) to turn the row off.** A scanned value that is only `UNION SELECT` or `UNION ALL SELECT` is flagged as `sqli`, as v4.4.0 did (the three bare fragments of the public SQLi corpus). The row is anchored on the whole value, so `how to union select two tables` stays clean. `flag_bare_union_select=False` drops only this row, for every parameter, and gives the 5.0.0 head from before this row (351 of the 354 UNION lines, v4.4.0 flags all 354); it does not restore any 4.x behaviour. `excluded_detection_params={"q": ["sqli"]}` is the per-parameter opt-out for a free-text field and drops every sqli row on that parameter.
- **A bare `UNION SELECT` that ends in `;`, `#`, a comment or a NUL, or starts with a slash, is flagged again (LEDGER 6.187).** v4.4.0 flagged `union select;`, `union select ;`, `UNION ALL SELECT` and `/union%20select` on a query parameter, a header or the URL path, and the anchored row of the previous entry missed all 21 of those forms. The same row now also allows a leading slash or byte-order mark and trailing whitespace, `;`, `#`, NUL and `/* ... */` comments around the two keywords, still on the whole value, so `how to union select two tables` stays clean. `flag_bare_union_select=False` turns all of them off.
- **The `flag_bare_union_select` switch and the per-app detection settings are read from the request's own config (LEDGER 6.186).** Two apps on one Redis prefix share one pattern manager that each request re-configured, so an app with the switch on could let a bare `union select` through while the other app (switch off) was serving a request at the same time (2000 of 2000 gathered requests in the probe). A request now scans with the config and detection state it was configured with, so `detection_compiler_timeout`, `detection_max_content_length`, `agent_enable_events` and `muted_event_types` are the request's own app's as well.
- **Threads of two apps on one Redis prefix no longer scan with the other app's detection state (LEDGER 6.202).** The request scope read the shared manager's detection state after `configure`, and with threads (Flask, Django) another app's `configure` could run in between, so an app could scan with the other app's semantic threshold, preprocessor and compiler (4308 of 20000 scopes in the probe, and `exec xp cmd shell` slipped through an app with `detection_semantic_threshold=0.05` in 5 of 3000 requests). `configure` and the capture of the request's scope now run under one lock held for that step only, never across the scan, so scans still overlap.
- **The bare `UNION SELECT` row is linear on a run of comments and flags every bare form v4.4.0 flagged (LEDGER 6.201).** `union select` followed by a run of `/**/` comments and one more character took 14 s of CPU at 28 comments (the scan's timeout cannot stop a regex that holds the GIL), and the row still missed `(union select)`, `\union select` and `/*x*/union select`. Every character of the value now has one way to match, so 2000 comments match in under a millisecond, and the row accepts any punctuation, whitespace, zero-width characters, digits and comments (a trailing one may be left open) around the two keywords, never a letter, so `how to union select two tables` stays clean. `flag_bare_union_select=False` turns the row off.
- **A `UNION SELECT` with a comment between its keywords is flagged again, as v4.4.0 flagged it (LEDGER 6.207).** `0 Union/**/ALL SELECT foo-- -`, `x' union /**/ sElEcT /**/ a-- -` and `1)Union/**/SELECT/*x*/'a'` were clean while their comment-free twins were flagged, so one inserted `/**/` hid the value. The row now accepts whitespace or a comment between `UNION`, `ALL` and `SELECT`, and up to three comments of up to 40 characters after `SELECT`; the bare `UNION SELECT` row also accepts an unclosed `/*` at the start of the value (`/*union select`). Comments are written so the regex engine keeps one stack frame per comment (a run of 3000 comments no longer holds 45 MB, and a 256 KB body of them costs a few MB per request); a comment body ends at its first slash, except between the keywords (next entry). A lone bare word as the select list (`union select foo`) stays clean, as decided.
- **A `UNION SELECT` with a line comment marker or a slash inside a comment between its keywords is flagged again, on the query and on a header, as v4.4.0 flagged it (LEDGER 6.211).** `# uniON#\nselect/*`, `1 UNION#\nSELECT 1,2` and `union --\nselect 1` (a `#` or `--` that ends a line, which the processed view reads as `#` or `--` followed by a space) and `1 UNION ALL/*a/b*/SELECT 1` (a comment whose body holds a slash) were clean on a header or on both surfaces. A gap between `UNION`, `ALL` and `SELECT` now accepts runs of whitespace, `#` and `-` that end in whitespace or a comment, and a comment body with a slash and no star; `#union #select` stays clean, as decided, because a gap never ends in a marker. A run of markers is one regex iteration, so a 256 KB body of repeated `#` followed by a space costs about 2 MB per request. A comment that opens with a slash and holds a star (`/*/* a */`) between the keywords is not accepted there; it still is before the keywords and after `SELECT`.
- **Fifty path rows match in linear time with no stack frame per path segment (LEDGER 6.212, 6.213).** Python's `re` keeps one stack frame per iteration of a repeated group, so the rows that walk a path prefix (the sensitive file, recon, CMS and directory traversal rows, about 45 of them) cost 70 to 83 bytes per character: a 256 KB text body of repeated `%/` raised the engine's peak memory by 184.8 MB on the previous landing and 842.0 MB on v4.4.0. Each of these rows now finds its candidate ends in one pass over the segments and builds its match from a counted flat pattern, so the same body raises peak memory by 31.6 MB (the rest is the percent decoder, not a row) and `etc/passwd` style rows go from 18.3 MB to 1.3 MB. The pair name and assignment scanners use one flat class loop instead of an alternation per character. Row text, required literals and every verdict are unchanged: the matches each row yields (span, every group span, string) equal the regex's on the fixture corpus, on near-miss mutations of every row's own tokens and on 2000 seeded strings per row, and the scan's CPU is charged to the pattern budget like any inline row.
- **The percent decoder, the scan-window helper and the row dispatch no longer cost memory or calls per character (LEDGER 6.212).** The standard library's `unquote` builds one bytes object per escape and joins them through a buffer table of 80 bytes per piece, so a 256 KB text body of repeated `%/` raised the engine's peak memory by 32.5 MB on the previous landing; the engine's decoder now joins its pieces in blocks of 4096 and returns the same text for every escape shape, mixed case, invalid escape and error mode (compared with `urllib.parse.unquote` on 30000 random strings and every one-byte escape), so the same body raises it by 6.1 MB. The scan-window helper keeps only the last terminator and an `array` of prefix starts instead of two lists of integers (a 256 KB body of `/` cost the `..;` row 9.6 MB of list). The two backtick helpers use an unrolled character loop with the same language. The per-row dispatch for linear rows is one dictionary lookup per row instead of three calls, so a request makes fewer calls than before the linear rows, and the finder walks segments with one regex step instead of two. Row text and every verdict are unchanged.
- **A second `initialize_redis_handlers` reports an unreachable ban threshold again when the Redis handler changed (LEDGER 6.188).** The route misconfiguration report was kept once per decorator revision, so a re-initialization with a different Redis handler (which changes whether the threshold is reachable) stayed silent. The handler is now part of what the report is remembered by.
- **A scan that only waited for a regex worker is no longer refused as an attack (LEDGER 6.198).** `detection_compiler_timeout` is the time a pattern runs in its worker. The wait for a free worker is bounded separately by the same value, and a scan that gets no worker in that time is incomplete: the request answers 503 with `Retry-After`, a `suspicious_request` event and no strike under `fail_secure=True` (LEDGER 6.481), and passes with the event under `fail_secure=False`, instead of a `pattern_timeout` threat that counted toward bans. A pattern that really uses its budget stays a `pattern_timeout` threat with its strike, now judged by the CPU the pattern itself used (a custom pattern by its worker's CPU, a built-in pattern by the scan's own CPU since its last yield, read only when the wall clock says it may be late) and no longer by the time since its scan began, so a harmless body that waited for the GIL under load is no longer refused (v4.4.0 judged wall time). A queue-wait expiry still counts toward replacing a pool held by stuck workers. `SusPatternsManager.detect()` and `detect_pattern_match()` raise `RegexQueueWaitError` (a `BodyReadFailedError`) when a scan gets no worker in time, and the enhanced check no longer falls back to the basic check on it.
- **`purge_dynamic_rules_on_start`** (`SecurityConfig`, `rule_id:version`, off by default) and the public `purge_dynamic_rules(rule_id, version)`. At every start, whatever `enable_dynamic_rules` says, a named set in the last-known snapshot is dropped locally: its owned bans are lifted (down to a shorter ban they hid, or off), its patterns removed, both snapshots deleted and a `dynamic_rules:purged` marker keeps every worker from applying it again until the SaaS answers a fresh check. One INFO line gives the counts and says auto-bans nested inside purged rule bans are cut back to their own expiry; a WARNING at every start says whether the setting matched. Ban ownership moves to the sorted set `dynamic_rules:owned_bans`, so two workers applying the same set both record their bans.
- **A ban write never shortens a live ban.** One script writes the later of the live and the new expiry for exact and CIDR bans (an explicit unban still lets a shorter ban follow), so a 60 s auto-ban no longer replaces a 24 h rule ban, and a rule ban over a shorter auto-ban lasts its own duration again, as in 4.4.0.
- **Revoking a dynamic rule lifts only the ban the rule wrote.** A rule that drops an IP or network compares each owned ban with the expiry the rule wrote (one script call per member, in memory when Redis is off): it deletes the ban only while it still holds that expiry, rewrites it to the shorter foreign ban the rule hid, and leaves a longer ban written over it alone (v4.4.0 never revoked, so none of these was ever lifted). The list comes from the shared `dynamic_rules:owned_bans` set, so a worker that restarted or never applied the earlier set still revokes it, and addresses and networks compare in canonical form. Strikes, recidive history and the grouped identity ban are never touched. One ban-version bump and one `ip_unbanned` event (`dynamic_rule_revoked`, with `outcome` and `remaining_seconds` when rewritten) per batch that lifted something; a batch that lifted nothing sends and bumps nothing. The whitelist path still unbans unconditionally.
- **The reference engine replays its own conformance corpus in CI, and the safety suite has 96 cases again.** The pytest step replays every suite in `specs/fixtures/cases/index.json` through `run_fixtures.py`, and the detection gate (normal and CPU-load shards) replays `safety_gates` with the timed classes accepting `probe_subprocess_timeout`, which a loaded host produces. The timing classes are fixed case IDs with their margins stored beside them: `over_budget` at cap 262144 (100x or more over the budget, replacing `safety_cost_verdict_over_budget_01`, which sat at 25x), `probe_string_timeout` and a deterministic `unreachable_probe`; the generator fails instead of dropping a case that leaves its class. The corpus tools state the staged 5.0.0 fields and publish them as `pipeline_defaults`, and the ecosystem gate runs tornadoapi-guard.

- **`ipv6_prefix_length` (`SecurityConfig`, default `64`, `48` to `128`): a native IPv6 client is one identity for rate limits, strikes and automatic bans.** Every address of the same /64 shares one rate-limit window, one set of strike counters and one ban, so rotating addresses inside an assigned /64 gains nothing and a ban lands on the whole prefix. IPv4 clients, Teredo and NAT64 addresses stay per address and 6to4 addresses group by their embedded IPv4 site. v4.4.0 keyed all of these by the single address, so an IPv6 client's windows and strikes restart once on upgrade. Restore the old behavior: `ipv6_prefix_length=128`. Every worker that shares one Redis prefix must use the same value: the first worker records it in `{redis_prefix}config:ipv6_prefix_length` and a worker whose value differs is reported through `fail_on_misconfiguration` (a startup failure by default), so a rolling change is not supported. Stop all workers, delete that key or use a new `redis_prefix`, then start them with one value.
- **`nat64_prefixes` (`SecurityConfig`, default empty) and the well-known NAT64 prefix decode to the client's IPv4 address.** A client that arrives as an IPv6 address inside `64:ff9b::/96`, or inside a prefix you list (RFC 6052 lengths /32, /40, /48, /56, /64 and /96), is attributed to the global IPv4 address embedded in it, so IPv4 lists, cloud ranges, bans and rate limits see the real client. An embedded non-global IPv4 address such as `10.0.0.5` is never unwrapped, and a list entry of another length or family is reported as a misconfiguration at startup and ignored. v4.4.0 treated every NAT64 address as an IPv6 client of its own. There is no switch for the well-known prefix, which is always decoded.
- **`country_rules_exempt_private_peers` (`SecurityConfig`, default `False`).** On, a private, link-local or carrier-grade-NAT peer is exempt from the country rules when its request carries no `X-Forwarded-For` header, and a peer that sends one is never exempt. It is off by default because behind Docker Desktop or rootless Docker every internet client reaches the app as a private peer without that header, so the exemption would switch the country rules off for them. Nothing changes unless you turn it on.
- **`block_empty_user_agent` (`SecurityConfig`, default `False`).** On, a request whose `User-Agent` header is absent, empty or blank is denied as a global user-agent denial: `403`, a `user_agent_blocked` event, and a hostile-signal strike under `ban_on_hostile_signals`. `exempt_ips` skip it like the other user-agent checks. It is off by default because some legitimate clients and health probes send no `User-Agent`.
- **`banned_ips_max_entries` (`SecurityConfig`, default `100000`, `>= 1`).** The bans one process keeps in memory are capped (v4.4.0 held at most 10,000; about 23 MiB of Python heap when full at the new default, measured with `python -m perf_gate.ban_store_memory`). At the cap the ban closest to expiry is evicted first, the first and every hundredth unexpired eviction is logged, and the eviction of a ban that has no Redis copy sends `ip_ban_evicted`. With Redis the bans themselves are not capped.
- **Five behavior tracking caps are `SecurityConfig` fields, and evicting a live entry sends `behavior_bucket_evicted`.** `behavior_max_tracked_endpoints`, `behavior_max_tracked_clients_per_endpoint`, `behavior_throttle_max_tracked_buckets`, `behavior_throttle_max_tracked_clients_per_bucket` and `behavior_throttle_max_trip_markers` (each `>= 1`, default `10000`, the values v4.4.0 hard-coded) bound the in-memory behavior-rule and throttle tracking. When a cap drops a live bucket or client, that client's count restarts, so a usage, frequency or return rule threshold can be missed and a throttled client is admitted again; dropping a trip marker only reports a running throttle episode as new. The engine sends one `behavior_bucket_evicted` event per `kind` (`endpoint`, `endpoint_client`, `throttle_bucket`, `throttle_client`, `trip_marker`) per minute, carrying `suppressed_count`, `store` and `outcome`, and one sampled WARNING. The Redis per-client rule-hit index, sized by `behavior_max_tracked_endpoints`, evicts nothing when it is full: the hit still counts for its own rule and only the client's cross-rule recent-hits total misses it, reported as `kind` `rule_hit_index` with `outcome` `refused`. guard-agent and the SaaS must accept the new event type.
- **`emergency_whitelist_only` (`SecurityConfig`, default `True`).** In emergency mode `True` admits only the `emergency_whitelist` clients and answers every other client `503`, as before. `False` keeps the lockdown and also admits `whitelist`, `exempt_ips` and the `ip_whitelist` entries of the active dynamic rule. The dynamic rule payload carries the same field and a payload without it keeps `True`, so no upgrade weakens a lockdown.
- **A custom Redis handler is checked at startup: `RedisHandlerProtocol` declares every method the engine calls, and `RedisHandlerBase` carries the defaults.** v4.4.0's protocol left three of them out. A handler you pass as `redis_handler` that lacks a method the engine calls is now reported by name through `fail_on_misconfiguration` (a startup failure by default) instead of failing on the first request that reaches it. Subclass the new public `guard_core.RedisHandlerBase`, which carries `RedisManager`'s own implementations, or implement the missing methods; `get_connection()` must yield a redis-py compatible client.
- **`detection_exclusion(path_params=...)` clears a probe token inside a route parameter, and `detection_exclusion(body_fields=...)` and `excluded_detection_body_fields` take `{field: {categories}}` like `excluded_detection_params`.** `path_params` is a set of route parameter names or a `{name: {categories}}` map. A probe token (`README.md`, a script extension, a named probe segment) that lies wholly inside a declared parameter's captured span, read from the matched route template and the path, is the app's own route, so `/github/file/owner/repo/master/README.md` on a `:filepath{.+}` route is clean once `path_params={"filepath"}` is declared. The probe rows keep running on the rest of the path, and the value rules (traversal, SQL injection, command injection) always run on the captured value. The set form clears only the recon and `cms_probing` rows, so a sensitive-file or traversal row is skipped only when the map names its category. A declared parameter with no literal segment before it (a root capture such as `/{path:path}`) is reported through `fail_on_misconfiguration`. With nothing declared the verdicts are v4.4.0's.
- **A response that holds its body in memory gives a body-reading `custom_response_modifier` the real response.** An adapter can declare the optional `body_is_materialized` capability on its response wrapper, and the four Python adapters do, from the framework's own streaming signal read before anything touches the body. When it is `True` the modifier gets the response exactly as in v4.4.0: the real `.body` bytes, one call, no prefetch, no replay and no `custom_response_modifier_max_body_bytes` cap, because the bytes are already in memory. A response that does not declare it, or declares `False` (a file or streaming response, a custom or third-party response class), takes the bounded path of the previous `custom_response_modifier` entry.
- **`security_headers_manager.configure(..., override_apps=True)` changes the security headers and CORS of every guarded app.** A `configure(...)` call sets exactly what it passes: a key it does not pass is absent from the manager's own headers and is not overridden for the apps. Without `override_apps=True` it changes only the manager's own headers and reaches no guarded app, because the middlewares and every released adapter call it at startup with every key filled in, and that call stays inert for the apps, as in 4.4.0. With `override_apps=True` the keys the call passes override the `security_headers` config of every guarded app in the process from the next response on, `None` switches that header off for every app, a passed `enabled` wins in both directions, and `configure(override_apps=True)` with nothing else, or `reset()`, clears the override. With Redis the override is stored and the other workers that run the same startup config pull it at most once per 5 s; a Redis error keeps the last applied override and retries after 5 s. `enable_cors`, `cors_max_age` and `cors_expose_headers` join the CORS keys of the override, and a merge that would give wildcard origins together with credentials keeps the app's own CORS with a WARNING and an event. The `cors_preflight` entry below says what an app built with CORS off does with it.

### Changed

- **`tzdata` is a runtime dependency (LEDGER 6.320, D-205).** `@time_window`, `time_restrictions` and the time-window check resolve a named zone such as `US/Eastern` through `zoneinfo`, which reads the system zone database. A slim container image, a Windows host or a minimal distribution has none, so every named zone was reported as not a known IANA time zone and a window with a zone denied every request. guard-core now depends on `tzdata` (the IANA database as a package), which `zoneinfo` falls back to when the system has none. No setting changes.
- **`GuardResponse.headers` is typed as `GuardResponseHeaders`, the members the engine uses, instead of `MutableMapping[str, str]`.** The protocol (`guard_core.protocols.GuardResponseHeaders`) holds `__getitem__`, `__setitem__`, `__delitem__`, `__contains__`, `get`, `keys` and `items`, so Werkzeug `Headers`, Django `ResponseHeaders`, Starlette `MutableHeaders`, Tornado `HTTPHeaders` and a plain `dict[str, str]` satisfy it without a `cast` (Werkzeug iterates `(name, value)` pairs and a Django response iterates its body, so neither is a `MutableMapping`; iteration and `len` are not part of it). Runtime behaviour is unchanged.
- **A Redis outage at request time answers 503 with `Retry-After: 5`, not 500** (release note, behavior change). Under `fail_secure=True` with `redis_fail_open=False` (the defaults), a `GuardRedisError` raised by a check now answers the status the error carries (503, as at startup) with a `Retry-After: 5` header; every other check error and a failed pipeline rebuild still answer 500, and passive mode, `fail_secure=False` and `redis_fail_open=True` behave as before. Excluded paths still run the ban lookup, so a health probe on one sees the 503 during an outage; `redis_fail_open=True` is the switch that admits it. Monitors that treat 500 as the signal for a Redis outage must also accept 503.
- **The Geo-IP lookup reads the IPinfo Lite schema as well as the legacy one.** `IPInfoManager.get_country` returns the ISO code from `country_code` when the record has it (IPinfo Lite, where `country` is the country name) and from `country` otherwise (the legacy `country_asn.mmdb`, unchanged). Before, a Lite file made country rules compare a name such as "Germany" against ISO codes and silently stop matching.
- **The GeoIP database download is bounded** (D-90, LEDGER 6.57). Each of the 3 attempts to download the database from ipinfo.io gives up after 30 s to connect, 30 s without a byte, or 300 s in total, on both engines, whatever way the endpoint stalls (a trickled header, a body that never decodes, a silent resolver, a stalling handshake or an HTTP, HTTPS, CONNECT or SOCKS proxy), with a `TimeoutError` as the outcome. A hung ipinfo.io used to hold the first requests for about 15 minutes (v4.4.0 equal). The 3 attempts, the 1 s and 2 s sleeps and the 60 s retry back-off are unchanged, and after the last attempt fails `blocked_countries` allows and `whitelist_countries` denies, as before. The sync tree now needs `urllib3>=2.7.0` and `aiohttp>=3.13.3` is the floor whose parser bounds `auto_decompress`.
- **`custom_request_check` and `custom_response_modifier` follow the callable's type in both engines, and `SecurityConfig` is generic in the request type.** The async engine awaits an async callable exactly as before and now also runs a sync callable inline on the event loop (it must not block). The sync engine runs a sync callable; an async callable raises `guard_core.exceptions.CallableShapeError` (a `TypeError` naming the field) when the middleware starts, and a sync callable that returns an awaitable raises it on its first result with the coroutine closed. `SecurityConfig[RequestT]` defaults to `GuardRequest`, so existing code is unchanged. WSGI adopters who run mypy write `SecurityConfig[SyncGuardRequest](...)` (mypy infers it from a sync check only without the pydantic mypy plugin; with the plugin a plain `SecurityConfig(custom_request_check=<sync check>)` is typed `SecurityConfig[GuardRequest]` and raises no mypy error, so write the parameter out); a config of one tree handed to the other tree is a type error. `auth_verifier` is typed through the same parameter. `pickle` and `copy.deepcopy` of a `SecurityConfig` now work.
- **Global `enforce_https` applies on every route, whatever its decorators.** A route with only a rate limit or a content filter used to be exempt from `enforce_https=True` because any route config replaced the global setting; it is now redirected like an undecorated route. `@require_https` still adds HTTPS on a route when the global setting is off. Behavior change: HTTP requests to such decorated routes are now redirected.
- **The legacy `RequestValidator.check_time_window` gives the same verdict as the time-window check, time zone included.** It evaluated UTC only; both now share one function, so the window end is minute-inclusive in every zone, DST days included.
- **A time-window evaluation error denies the request instead of allowing it.** A route whose `time_restrictions` cannot be evaluated (a missing `start` or `end`, a non-string value, a time zone that fails to load, an exception from the clock) is answered exactly like a request outside the window (`403 Access not allowed at this time`, same log line, `decorator_violation` event and strike), with one ERROR log that names the restriction and the error class and carries no request data. A time zone that fails at runtime no longer falls back to UTC. `RequestValidator.check_time_window` gives the same verdict. A malformed `HH:MM` string or an unknown zone is still rejected when the `@time_window` decorator runs; values set directly on a `RouteConfig` skip that check, and an unknown zone there now denies.
- **`blocked_user_agents` patterns (global, route and dynamic) see the whole `User-Agent` header, not its first 512 characters.** Padding a header past 512 characters no longer hides a blocked name. Patterns still pass the ReDoS validator at registration (unchanged, no pattern is rejected). The header is searched in 512-character windows that overlap by 256, in place, so `^`, `$`, `\b` and lookaheads keep their meaning, and a match of up to 256 characters gives the verdict of a full-string search. Matching cost grows linearly with the header length.
- **A raising auth verifier is reported as a verifier error, not as an attack** (release note, behavior change). 4.x answered `401 Authentication required` and logged nothing of the cause; 5.0.0 answers `503 Authentication service unavailable` without a challenge, logs the cause at ERROR with the credential redacted and counts no strike, and the request no longer writes the `suspicious` "Authentication failure" log line or sends a `decorator_violation` or `authentication_failed` event; it sends one new `auth_verifier_error` event (`logged_only` in passive mode, `reason`, `auth_type` and `violation_type` in the metadata). guard-agent and the SaaS must accept the new event type.
- **A missing API key answers 401, not 400** (release note, behavior change). A request without the `api_key_auth` key used to be stopped by the required-headers check with `400 Missing required header` and a `required_header` strike; the authentication check now answers it with `401` and `WWW-Authenticate: ApiKey`, and counts as `auth_failure`, so per-category counts and thresholds see it there.
- **The time-based SQLi row accepts a decimal delay** (release note, wider than 4.4.0). `SLEEP(2.5)`, `pg_sleep(0.5)` and the code forms `time.sleep(0.5)` or `Thread.sleep(0.5)` are flagged exactly when their integer twin is flagged; 4.4.0 flagged only an integer delay, so `1 AND SLEEP(2.5)` passed. A benign text that was clean with an integer stays clean with a decimal.
- **5.0.0 flips `ban_on_hostile_signals` to `True`** (release note, behavior change). Failed authentication, missing required headers, bad referrers, block-list hits and clear `X-Forwarded-For` forgery now count toward automatic IP bans when the flag is left unset, so a client that keeps failing a gate gets banned at `auto_ban_threshold`. Restore the old behavior: `ban_on_hostile_signals=False`.
- **5.0.0 flips `fail_on_misconfiguration` to `True`** (release note, behavior change). A misconfigured config or route decorator (a `rate_limit` below 1, a zero detection threshold, a glob in `exclude_paths`, an empty `require_ip` whitelist and the rest of the list in the field description) now raises a `ValueError` and the app does not start; before, it started (a glob in `exclude_paths`, an unknown `security_headers` key and a few more logged one WARNING, a `rate_limit` below 1 and a zero detection threshold logged nothing), and every message keeps its text. A `rate_limit`, `rate_limit_window` or `endpoint_rate_limits` entry below 1 is refused only while `enable_rate_limiting` is on. Restore the old behavior: `fail_on_misconfiguration=False`.
- **A country rule or a geo rate limit with no geo source now fails at declaration** (release note, behavior change). `@block_countries`, `@allow_countries` and `@geo_rate_limit` raise `ValueError` when they are applied (at import) and the decorator's config has neither `geo_ip_handler` nor `ipinfo_token`, or has `ipinfo_token` but not the `maxminddb` package and the tree's downloader (`aiohttp` or `requests`), checked without any I/O. Before, the same mistake started the app and failed at the first request (lazy adapters) or at the first `initialize_redis_handlers` call. It applies only while `fail_on_misconfiguration` is `True`; with `False` nothing happens at declaration and the init report warns once, as before. Setups that change: a decorator built from a config without a geo source but paired with a middleware config that has `ipinfo_token` now raises at import (give the decorator's config the token or the handler). `HandlerInitializer.report_route_misconfigurations()` is new, public and I/O-free: it holds the warning-level route reports (country rules without GeoIP, unreachable ban thresholds, route limits under a disabled global switch), `initialize_redis_handlers` calls it, and a second call for the same decorator state reports nothing, so djapi-guard calls it when the middleware is built. When the decorator's config has `fail_on_misconfiguration=False` and the middleware's config has it True, the startup report for the route's country rule follows the decorator's config: one warning, no refusal on every request.
- **5.0.0: a `/` entry in `exclude_paths` covers only the root page** (release note, behavior change). It used to exclude every check for the whole application and was reported as a misconfiguration; it no longer is. Restore the whole-application exclusion: `exclude_all_paths=True`.
- **5.0.0: a request body past `detection_max_body_inspect_bytes` is never passed unscanned** (release note, behavior change). A body up to the cap is scanned in full as before. A body past it, on an adapter with a bounded reader, had only its first 256 KiB scanned and the rest passed; it now gets the body-read failure outcome after the prefix scan: `400` under `fail_secure`, a `suspicious_request` event and no strike, and with `fail_secure=False` it passes on the prefix scan with the event. A threat inside the prefix stays a threat. A route whose `max_request_size` is above the global cap uses that value as its inspect cap, so its whole body is scanned while a sibling route without it is refused; `max_request_size` also sizes the read buffer, so raise it only as far as the memory per request you accept. Restore the old pass: `fail_secure=False`. The default cap (`262144`) is unchanged here and is set separately.
- **5.0.0: a multipart upload past 1 MiB is refused with 400 unless its limit is raised, through the new `detection_max_file_inspect_bytes`** (release note, behavior change). A request whose `Content-Type` is `multipart/form-data` is inspected up to `detection_max_file_inspect_bytes`; every other body keeps `detection_max_body_inspect_bytes`. The body limit defaults to `262144` and the file limit to `1048576` (1 MiB), so a multipart upload over 1 MiB on a route without a larger `max_request_size` now answers `400` under `fail_secure` (the default), with a `suspicious_request` event and no strike, instead of passing with only its first 256 KiB scanned. Raise it for every upload with `SecurityConfig(detection_max_file_inspect_bytes=5_000_000)` (range 1024 to 10485760), or for one route with `@guard_deco.max_request_size(5 * 1024 * 1024)`, which makes that route's inspect limit at least that value for both kinds of body. For example, an upload route that takes images up to 5 MB: `@app.post("/upload")` with `@guard_deco.max_request_size(5 * 1024 * 1024)` on it scans the whole 5 MB file, while `/comments` keeps the global limits. The limit sizes the read buffer per request, so raise it only as far as the memory per upload you accept; `fail_secure=False` restores the old pass, with the event. A multipart body past the limit gets the same outcome as any body past `detection_max_body_inspect_bytes`: a prefix scan first (a threat inside it stays a threat), then the refusal, never a prefix pass.
- **5.0.0: the multipart inspect limit defaults to 1 MiB, and what it costs** (release note, behavior change). `detection_max_file_inspect_bytes` defaults to `1048576`. A multipart upload up to it is scanned in full; past it, under `fail_secure` (the default), the request answers `400` with a `suspicious_request` event and no strike, and the log line and the event detail name the field to raise (`detection_max_file_inspect_bytes` for multipart, `detection_max_body_inspect_bytes` for other bodies) and the per-route `max_request_size`. Raise it for every upload with `SecurityConfig(detection_max_file_inspect_bytes=5_000_000)`, or for one route with `@guard_deco.max_request_size(5 * 1024 * 1024)`, which makes that route's inspect limit at least that value. Measured with 64 read slots busy: 0.66 to 0.85 CPU seconds per MB of text multipart and 0.10 to 0.18 per MB of binary multipart (v4.4.0's 0.0114 is not equal work: it scanned a smaller window). Memory is about 7.5 to 13.8 MB per in-flight 1 MiB upload, and the 64-slot peak at 1 MiB was 386.6 to 908.5 MB. The async tree has no read-slot cap by default (`async_body_read_max_concurrent` is `None`), so its memory is that per-upload figure times the uploads in flight. The scan window and the pattern validation cap follow the larger of `detection_max_body_inspect_bytes` and `detection_max_file_inspect_bytes`, so lowering only the body limit does not lower multipart. A `Content-Type` is multipart only when its media type is `multipart/form-data` (the part before `;`, case-insensitive): `application/json; x=multipart/form-data` gets the body limit. A route's `max_request_size`, which v4.4.0 used only for the 413 gate, now also sets how many bytes are inspected when it is above the applicable limit; measured, that costs about 0.7 to 0.85 CPU seconds per MB of text and, on the async tree, an event-loop stall of about 0.18 s per MB of clean body (0.37 s at 2 MB, 0.71 to 0.74 s at 4 MB). I make no claim that typical uploads fit within 1 MiB.
- **5.0.0: `enable_redis` is no longer on by default** (release note, behavior change). Left unset, Redis is on exactly when one of `redis_url`, `redis_prefix`, `redis_socket_connect_timeout`, `redis_socket_timeout`, `redis_health_check_interval`, `redis_max_connections`, `redis_pool_timeout`, `redis_retries` or `redis_fail_open` was set explicitly (at construction or by assignment before the middleware binds), and off otherwise, so an app that never configured Redis stops connecting to a Redis on localhost. An explicit `enable_redis` always wins, and a falsy `redis_url` means no Redis at every use site (before, `redis_url=""` built a client in one place and not in another). Restore the old default: `enable_redis=True`.
- **5.0.0: `redis`, `maxminddb`, `aiohttp` and `requests` are no longer installed with guard-core** (release note, behavior change). The base install carries only `cachetools`, `pydantic` and `typing-extensions`. Redis, country rules and cloud-provider blocking need their extra: the `redis`, `geo` (`maxminddb`, plus the downloader of the built-in geo manager) and `cloud` (`aiohttp` and `requests`) extras of guard-core; each adapter forwards the same extras (`fastapi-guard[redis]`, `flaskapi-guard[geo]`, `djapi-guard[cloud]`, `tornadoapi-guard[redis]`). Every import of the four is lazy, so `import guard_core` and an adapter's top-level import work without them, and an app that sets no Redis field, no country rule and no cloud blocking runs on the base install. Configuring a feature whose package is missing raises the `SecurityConfig` validation error at construction, and now also on assignment, `model_copy` and a dynamic-rule batch, naming `guard-core[<extra>]` and the adapter's own extra. The cloud and geo checks follow the tree: the sync adapters need `requests`, the async ones `aiohttp`, so a Flask app with only `aiohttp` is refused for cloud blocking: `SecurityConfig` accepts either downloader, and the middleware refuses at startup with `DownloaderNotInstalledError` naming the tree's own downloader (`guard_core/core/initialization/_handler_initializer_steps.py`, `_require_tree_downloader`). Anyone who relied on the base install pulling the four in must add the extra, or install the packages themselves.
- **5.0.0: the default rate limit is 120 requests per 60 s** (release note, behavior change), up from 10. Restore the old limit: `rate_limit=10`.
- **5.0.0: `whitelist_access_only` defaults to `True`** (release note, behavior change). The global `whitelist` only admits an IP: a whitelisted IP is rate limited, user-agent and cloud checked and scanned for attacks like any other client, collects hostile-signal strikes and identity escalation, and a whitelisted address or CIDR can be banned and the ban is enforced. Hosts that need the skips belong in `exempt_ips`. Emergency mode keeps its own whitelist, `request.state.is_whitelisted` is `False` and `request.state.whitelist_entry` names the matching entry, and `fastapi-guard`'s WebSocket guard follows the flag. Restore the old behavior: `whitelist_access_only=False`.
- **5.0.0: a capitalized built-in auth type passes the verifier only the credential** (release note, behavior change). `require_auth("Bearer")` and `require_auth("Basic")` (any case) already got the built-in prefix check, challenge and log redaction; their verifier now receives the token or the Basic blob, as for the lowercase types, instead of the whole `Authorization` header. Each such route logs one WARNING at registration. A verifier that parses the whole header must read the credential; there is no flag, write the lowercase type to say it plainly.
- **5.0.0: `CloudIpStoreFactory` is typed `Callable[[RedisHandlerProtocol | None], CloudIpStoreProtocol]`** (release note, typing only). A custom `cloud_ip_store` factory receives `None` when Redis is off, as it always did at runtime, and the alias now says so. A factory annotated with a bare `RedisHandlerProtocol` parameter no longer type-checks against it: widen the parameter to `RedisHandlerProtocol | None`. Runtime behavior is unchanged.
- **5.0.0: CORS values outside their grammar or with control characters are refused** (release note, behavior change). `cors_allow_origins` entries must be `*`, `null` or `scheme://host[:port]` with an optional trailing `/`, and `cors_allow_methods`, `cors_allow_headers` and `cors_expose_headers` entries must each be one RFC 9110 token (one method or header name per entry, no spaces, commas or control characters). A config that breaks this raises a `ValueError` when it is built or when the field is assigned. A remote CORS override that breaks it keeps the app's own value for that field, logs one warning and reports the existing `cors_override_refused` reason. Every emitted header value also refuses characters a response cannot carry (DEL and anything above U+00FF). Split a comma-joined entry such as `"Content-Type, Authorization"` into one entry per name.
- **5.0.0: `SecurityConfig(security_headers=...)` refuses a header value or name the response cannot carry when it is built or assigned** (release note, behavior change). A custom header name must be one RFC 9110 token, and every header value (`frame_options`, `permissions_policy`, the other named headers and the `custom` values) must be at most 8192 characters, hold no CR or LF, and hold only tab, space, printable ASCII and U+0080 to U+00FF once control characters below U+0020 are stripped, so U+2028, U+2029, DEL and anything above U+00FF raise a `ValidationError` at construction (a `ValueError` on assignment). Before this the same value built fine and failed at the first response or at `configure()`. A block with `enabled: false` is not checked, as no response reads it. The messages of the response-time checks are unchanged. A setting of the wrong shape is refused the same way, with the message the response path uses: a named header that is not a string, a `csp` or `hsts` that is not a mapping, and a `custom` block that is not a mapping of string names to string values.
- **5.0.0: `GuardResponse.headers` is typed with the structural `GuardResponseHeaders` protocol** (release note, typing only). It names the members the engine and the adapters use (`__getitem__`, `__setitem__`, `__delitem__`, `__contains__`, `get`, `keys`, `items`), so werkzeug `Headers`, Django `ResponseHeaders`, Starlette `MutableHeaders`, Tornado `HTTPHeaders` and a plain `dict` satisfy it without a cast. An adapter whose headers object is a `MutableMapping` keeps type-checking. Runtime behavior is unchanged.
- **5.0.0: `GuardRequest.headers` is typed with the structural `GuardRequestHeaders` protocol** (release note, typing only). It names the members the engine and the adapters use (`__getitem__`, `__contains__`, `get`, `keys`, `items`), so werkzeug `EnvironHeaders` (which iterates `(name, value)` pairs and is not a `Mapping`), Starlette `Headers`, Django `HttpHeaders`, Tornado `HTTPHeaders` and a plain `dict` satisfy it without a `cast` or a runtime check in the adapter. Every `Mapping[str, str]` still satisfies it; iteration and `len` are not part of it. Runtime behavior is unchanged.
- **5.0.0: route decorators keep the decorated function's signature for type checkers** (release note, typing only). Every route decorator is typed `Callable[[Callable[P, R]], Callable[P, R]]` through the new `GuardedParams` (ParamSpec) and `GuardedReturn` (TypeVar), and `GuardedCallable` is the alias `Callable[GuardedParams, GuardedReturn]`; a non-callable argument is now a type error. The decorated object is typed as a callable, so code that read `decorated._guard_route_id` through the type checker reads it with `get_route_id(decorated)`; the attribute is still set at runtime. Runtime behavior is unchanged.
- **5.0.0: `SecurityConfig.security_headers` is typed `dict[str, object] | None`** (release note, typing only). It was `dict[str, Any] | None`, so a nested key such as `hsts` could be read and indexed without a check; the type checker now sees `object` there. Code that read a nested key through the type checker narrows it first: `hsts = block.get("hsts"); hsts_config = hsts if isinstance(hsts, dict) else {}`. Runtime behavior is unchanged.
- **5.0.0: a partial `security_headers` block sends `X-XSS-Protection: 0`, like the defaults** (release note, behavior). A block that leaves `xss_protection` out now takes the default value `0` (the partial block merges over the defaults); the adapters' settings readers fell back to `1; mode=block`, the 4.x default. A block that sets `xss_protection` keeps its value.
- **A startup WARNING lists the 5.0.0 defaults left unset, each flip once per configuration.** When the config is built, when one of the fields that decide a flip is assigned later (`exclude_paths`, `exclude_all_paths`, `whitelist`, `whitelist_access_only`, `rate_limit`, `enable_rate_limiting`, `enable_redis` and the nine Redis connection fields) and when the middleware binds, a WARNING names each changed default that applies to the config and has not been logged yet for this configuration (`ban_on_hostile_signals`, `fail_on_misconfiguration`, `rate_limit` while rate limiting is on, `whitelist_access_only` while a whitelist is set, `enable_redis` with the field that kept Redis on when it did, a `/` entry in `exclude_paths` while `exclude_all_paths` is not set), each with the line that restores the old behavior. A later evaluation that finds new flips logs one more WARNING with only those; a flip is never logged twice, and a config that states every one of them logs nothing. It is a log line only, no `warnings.warn`. The staged 4.5.0 `DeprecationWarning`s are gone. A misconfiguration logged because `fail_on_misconfiguration=False` names the line that builds the config or registers the route.
- **IP lists match through one cached index, and the X-Forwarded-For spoof check is bounded** (release notes). The whitelist, blacklist, `exempt_ips`, route lists, emergency whitelist and `trusted_proxies` no longer parse every CIDR on every request (1000 entries: 4100 us to 12 us); verdicts are unchanged, an invalid entry still raises when it is reached before a match. The spoof check and the spoof warning and event of an untrusted public peer inspect at most 32 `X-Forwarded-For` entries from the left, bounded before parsing, because a 16 KiB header holds about 2,000 (the check cost 28 ms with no whitelist and 500 ms with 100 whitelist CIDRs per request, now 0.2 ms); entries past the 32nd never count toward a ban, and the message names the rest as `... and N more`. The right-to-left walk that resolves the client behind trusted proxies is not bounded.
- **Ban store changes with Redis on** (release notes). A worker keeps the local copy of a Redis-backed ban for at most 3600 s (`LOCAL_CACHE_TTL_CAP_SECONDS`, as 4.4.0 did) whatever the ban's duration, so a key deleted from Redis without moving the ban version (a port or script that deletes it) stops being enforced within an hour; a ban held only in memory keeps its full duration. `ip_ban_evicted` is sent for every eviction of a ban that has no Redis copy (Redis off, or kept in this process after a failed Redis write) and not for a Redis-backed one, which the next lookup reads again; the WARNING stays sampled. The ban and unban writes and the ban version counter are applied in one MULTI/EXEC, so a lost connection leaves neither a new ban other workers never reload nor a lifted ban they keep. A `whitelist_access_only` warning is also evaluated when the config is bound, so a whitelist assigned after construction warns once.
- **Behavior counters are kept per client identity** (release note: behavior windows for IPv6 clients restart once). Every behavior key, counter and reader (`behavior:usage:*`, `behavior:return:*`, `behavior:throttle:*`, the recent-event index, `get_recent_event_count`, `count_recent_rule_hits`, the throttle episode marker) and a behavioral `ban` rule now use the client's identity: its IPv6 network at `ipv6_prefix_length` (a /64 by default) instead of the address. IPv4 clients and `ipv6_prefix_length=128` produce the same keys as 4.4.0 byte for byte, and the identity text is the canonical address (a `::ffff:` peer is its IPv4). A native IPv6 client moves to its network's key on upgrade, so its behavior windows restart once and neighbours in one /64 now share them. A behavioral `ban` rule bans the identity and, with `enable_recidive`, escalates and counts like a threshold ban.
- **A rejected request is no longer recorded in the rate-limit window** (release note). A request blocked by any tier is recorded in none, so a flooding client does not grow its own window (300 blocked requests used to leave 300 members) and a client that keeps retrying is unblocked once its allowed hits expire. The block still happens at the same request: the `limit`-th request passes and the next one is blocked.
- **The rate-limit auto-ban counts violations across workers when Redis is on.** The threshold is the configured number of violations whatever the worker count (4 workers used to need 29 429s for a threshold of 8), the counter survives a restart and is cleared when the ban lands. Rate-limit violations and detection hits are counted apart: a flat `auto_ban_threshold` for a rate-limit ban counts only rate-limit violations and the flat threshold of a detection ban never counts them.
- **Excluded paths and CORS preflights skip rate limiting.** `exclude_paths` entries (health probes, static assets) no longer use up the client's budget or feed the rate-limit auto-ban, and an `OPTIONS` preflight is never counted. Bans and the IP, country and cloud lists still apply to both.
- **`enable_rate_limiting=False` turns off the global limit only.** `@rate_limit`, `@geo_rate_limit` and `endpoint_rate_limits` stay on, and startup logs one INFO line naming the routes whose limits stay on.
- **Redis rate-limit keys.** The route, geo and endpoint tiers write `{prefix}rate_limit:rate:{ip}:{kind}:{sha256(bucket)}` (`kind` is `endpoint`, `route` or `geo`), the script reads the clock from Redis `TIME` and checks every tier of a request in one call, and the violation counter is `{prefix}rate_limit:violations:{ip}`. Redis 5 or later (or `redis.replicate_commands()`, which the script calls when it exists) is needed for `TIME` in a script. A mixed 4.4.0 and 4.5.0 fleet on one Redis counts the two key shapes apart until every worker is upgraded.
- **Redis connection pool.** The client uses a pool of `redis_max_connections` connections (100 when unset): a free connection is taken at once and only an exhausted pool waits, up to `redis_pool_timeout`, so a burst above the pool size waits instead of failing (200 concurrent requests used to give 100 `GuardRedisError`).
- **One rate-limit manager per config object.** `RateLimitManager(config)` returns the live manager for that config object, so the middleware and `rate_limit_handler(config)` called with the app's own config hold the same windows, Redis and agent handlers. Different config objects (equal ones and `model_copy()` included) get their own manager, and the class and its constructor signature are unchanged.
- **The detection auto-ban counts across workers when Redis is on.** Every detection strike (`threat_ban_config` categories and the flat `auto_ban_threshold`, hostile-signal bans included) is also recorded in Redis per IP and per category over the same 3600 s sliding window, in one round trip per strike, so the threshold is the configured number of detections whatever the worker count (4 workers banned after 9, 6 and 10 detections for a threshold of 5). Each key is bounded to 10,000 members, a landed ban clears the IP's shared counters, a Redis error falls back to the process count with a WARNING, passive mode never counts, and a benign request costs no Redis command. Keys: `{prefix}detection:strikes:{ip}`, `{prefix}detection:strikes:{ip}|{category}` and `{prefix}detection:strikes_index:{ip}`. `middleware.suspicious_request_counts` stays populated.
- **`RateLimitManager.reset()` keeps the Redis and agent handlers attached and clears the rate-limit strikes.** It clears the request windows (memory and the Redis `rate_limit:rate:*` keys) and the rate-limit auto-ban strikes (process-wide memory and the Redis `rate_limit:violations:*` keys), and leaves the detection strikes and the active bans alone. On a config object no guarded app was built with it resets the guarded apps built with an equal config (one WARNING), else clears the Redis windows and strikes through a short-lived client, else warns. After a reset the app's limiter used to count per worker and send no rate-limit events until restart. A teardown is a new config object or a re-initialization, not `reset()`.
- **Every reader of the strike counters sees the shared counts when Redis is on.** The `request_count` of `penetration_attempt` events and the correlated categories of `correlate_with_detection` rules (global return and throttle rules) now use the detection strikes and the rate-limit violations of every worker. The event count comes from the reply of the strike script (no extra command); the categories are read only when a correlated rule's count can change its outcome, so a request on a route without such a rule, or below the correlated threshold, costs no Redis command. `middleware.suspicious_request_counts` stays populated, and without Redis, in passive mode or when Redis fails the process counts are used as before.
- **No strike outlives its ban.** `unban_ip(ip)` clears that identity's strikes of both families (every guarded middleware's counts, the by-IP counter, the Redis rate-limit violations and detection strikes), `unban_inside` clears exactly the addresses it removed, and `ip_ban_manager.reset()` clears every strike in memory and Redis. Before, a manually unbanned IP could be banned again by one more strike.
- **Redis `KEYS` is gone from every prefix-wide clear and listing.** `keys()`, `delete_pattern()`, the rate-limit and ban resets and the range unban walk the keyspace with `SCAN` and delete with `UNLINK` in batches, so a clear no longer stalls every other client of a shared Redis. Return values are unchanged.
- **A whitelisted range resets the strikes of every identity it covers.** A dynamic rule `ip_whitelist` entry (one address, a `/32` or a wider range) clears the in-process and Redis strike counters of every address it covers once per changed rule, banned or not, and so does the startup `whitelist` once per deployment of that whitelist (a Redis fingerprint key per prefix, a 60 s lock, stored after the scan succeeded); before, only an exact address (and the addresses whose ban a range lifted) started fresh.
- **`excluded_detection_headers` skips an operator-listed header in every category.** A header you list (config or route) is no longer scanned at all, like `excluded_detection_params` and `excluded_detection_body_fields`; the built-in identity and proxy header set keeps the `ssrf`-only skip.
- **Field names are redacted by credential token in hit logs and event previews.** A name whose tokens include `pass`, `pwd`, `token`, `secret`, `session`, `sid`, `auth`, `key` or `otp` (split on delimiters and camelCase) has its value hidden; `keywords`, `author` and `monkey` stay visible.
- **`excluded_detection_params` accepts `{param: {categories}}`.** Only the listed categories are skipped for that parameter; a plain set still skips every category. Unknown categories and empty sets are rejected.
- **Custom patterns are stored in Redis twice, so a mixed fleet keeps working.** `patterns:custom` keeps the comma-joined value an earlier engine reads (a pattern with a comma is split by those engines, as before), and the new `patterns:custom_list` holds the exact patterns as a sorted JSON list, so a pattern with a comma survives a restart of this engine. Both keys are written in one MULTI. On start the list is used only when it agrees with what the legacy key splits into; when an earlier engine added or removed a pattern after this engine wrote, the legacy key wins and one WARNING says so.
- **Request bodies are read through `read_body_prefix_until_idle(max_bytes, idle_timeout)`, and a stalled or failed read gets `408` or `400`.** The new optional adapter method (`IdleBoundedBodyReader`, `SyncIdleBoundedBodyReader`) raises `BodyReadStalledError` when no byte arrives for `body_read_timeout` seconds and `BodyReadFailedError` when the read fails. With `fail_secure` on the request gets `408` or `400`, with a log line and a `suspicious_request` event and no strike; with it off the request is logged and passes. An adapter without the method still works: guard-core reads `read_body_prefix`, then `body`, in full with no deadline of its own and logs one warning that names the missing method, once per adapter class. `body_read_timeout` is now an idle timeout, not a total one. The sync tree reads on the request thread and no longer starts a daemon thread per read, so the engine never abandons a read half-way.
- **`async_body_read_max_concurrent` (new, default `None`) bounds the async body reads; `sync_body_read_max_concurrent` (default `64`) bounds the sync ones.** `None` means no limit, as in v4.4.0. When a limit is set and a read waits for a slot longer than `body_read_timeout`, the request gets the `fail_secure` outcome above (`408`, a log line and an event, no strike) instead of silently skipping detection. The async tree no longer reads `sync_body_read_max_concurrent`.
- **A page that looks like a probe is judged by the app's own routes (the route gate).** v4.4.0 flags a script path (`.php`, `.asp`, `.jsp`, `.py`, `.sh` and the other extensions) at any depth and a set of named paths (`/management`, `/system/version`, `/language/en`, `README.md`, `/en/inicio.html`, the CMS directories) wherever they sit; the earlier 4.5.0 builds narrowed those rows to root shapes and missed nested probes. They are back with v4.4.0's pattern text. In a URL path each is flagged by default and clean when the route template the adapter matched literally contains the probe token (the extension or the named segment); a catch-all or converter template carries no token, a parameter named like a probe token (`/{language}/news`, `/<language>/news`, `<str:language>/news`, a Tornado `(?P<language>[^/]+)`) does not declare it because only the template's literal text is tested, and an adapter that gives no route knowledge behaves exactly as v4.4.0. Adapters record the matched template as `request.state.guard_route_template` (fastapi-guard, flaskapi-guard and djapi-guard before the pipeline) or implement `route_template(path)` (tornadoapi-guard). A value in a query or body carries no route and keeps the v4.4.0 behavior, apart from the bare file names D-70 decided. Expansion over v4.4.0, behind the same gate: the alternate server extensions (`.phtml`, `.php3` to `.php8`, `.pht`, `.phps`, `.phar`, `.asa`, `.cer`, `.ashx`, `.asmx`, `.ascx`) and the tricks that make a server run a file under another name (`;params`, `%00`, a trailing dot, `::$DATA`, a trailing image extension such as `.php.jpg`). A `.ts` or `.map` file under a static-file mount (HLS segments, source maps) is clean when the adapter's `static_file_exists(path)` finds the file in the mount, with the framework's traversal-safe lookup and no cache; the other source and dump extensions stay flagged even when the file exists. `.jspx` is left out because it flags 255 paths of the Tomcat benign set, and a trailing `.txt` or `.pdf` is left out because it flags the pinned benign `/config.rb.txt` family.
- **A Referer or User-Agent that names an internal address skips the `ssrf` category only when the request's Host is internal.** A browser UI opened on localhost or a LAN address (Host `localhost:8000`) is clean; the same Referer to `169.254.169.254` or `127.0.0.1:6379` on a server whose Host is public is `ssrf` again, as in v4.4.0.
- **`honeypot_detection` inspects every request shape and a filled trap bans on the first hit** (release note, behavior change). It no longer skips methods other than POST, PUT and PATCH and now reads the query string, urlencoded and multipart bodies and JSON (nested objects and arrays, every copy of a duplicated key, UTF-8 with a BOM, UTF-16 and UTF-32). Trap field names and media types match case-insensitively, and any value other than null, an empty string or whitespace (`0`, `false` and `[]` included) trips the trap. A body the inspection cap or the JSON depth limit keeps from being inspected is logged at WARNING. A filled trap bans the client IP on the first hit through the ban path (`threat_ban_config` category `honeypot`, threshold 1, `auto_ban_duration`); passive mode only logs.
- **Every credential configured on a route is verified** (release note, behavior change). `require_auth` plus `api_key_auth` on one route used to run only the bearer verifier; each configured credential now runs its own verifier and a missing or bad one gives 401.
- **The 400 for a missing or wrong required header says `Bad request`** (release note, behavior change). It used to name the header and say whether a value was close; the reason stays in the log line and the event.
- **IP security and rate limiting run before the identity checks** (release note, behavior change). `required_headers`, `authentication`, `referrer`, `custom_validators` and `time_window` now run after `cloud_ip_refresh`, `ip_security`, `cloud_provider`, `user_agent` and `rate_limit`, so a banned, blocked or rate-limited client is answered before any verifier or validator runs and a client probing credentials is rate limited. A request from an address outside the whitelist gets the 403 where it used to get the 401 or 400.
- **A new `cors_preflight` check answers a CORS preflight for an app built with CORS off, once `configure(override_apps=True, enable_cors=True)` is called.** `configure()` also accepts `enable_cors`, `cors_max_age` and `cors_expose_headers` with the other CORS keys, a wildcard origin with credentials keeps the app's own CORS with a WARNING and an event, and the default pipeline lists one more check name.
- **`route_resolution_strict` answers `404` for a path no route serves and `405` for a path another method serves, and no longer blocks a CORS preflight when CORS is on** (release note, behavior change). It answered `500 Route resolution failed` for all three. The body text is unchanged and a failing route lookup still gives 500.
- **`rate_limit` and `geo_rate_limit` buckets are keyed by the decorated route's identity** (release note, behavior change). A templated route is one bucket per client across ids and spellings, and the `endpoint_path` passed to `check_rate_limit` is the route identity, not the raw request path. The hashed bucket input of `{prefix}rate_limit:rate:{ip}:{kind}:{sha256(bucket)}` changes, so old and new instances count in different buckets during a rolling upgrade.
- **The rate-limit counters are no longer a process singleton** (release note, behavior change). `RateLimitManager._instance` is gone and each middleware has its own manager, so a test fixture or script that reads or resets `RateLimitManager._instance` raises `AttributeError`. Use `rate_limit_handler(config)` with the app's own config object, which returns the live manager.
- **A global blacklist entry always denies, under any allow-list** (release note, behavior change). A route `require_ip(whitelist=...)` used to lift the global blacklist on that route and a global `whitelist` hid the blacklist, so a blacklisted address inside a whitelisted network was let in. The blacklist is now checked first, a route allow-list replaces only the global whitelist and `bypass(["ip"])` stays the one explicit override.
- **`bypass(["all"])` responses carry the security headers, and the bypass applies in passive mode** (release note, behavior change). A bypassed route used to answer with the downstream response and the response modifier only; it now goes through the same security-header pass as every other response, and with `passive_mode` on the bypass no longer runs the whole pipeline log-only.
- **Text that only names SQL or shell words, or shows `${...}`, `$(...)` and backticks as documentation, is no longer flagged** (release note, behavior change). Rows that v4.4.0 matched on the words alone now need the structure of an attack. `select * from the shelf` (a determiner after `FROM`), `or 1=1 is a tautology` (a copula after the comparison), `benchmark results for sleep(5) function docs` (`SLEEP(n)` needs a SQL keyword, punctuation or the start of the value before it), `union select committee` (a plain name after `SELECT`) and `curl is a tool; wget is another` (an English function word after the command) are clean. A `${...}` counts as `cmd_injection` only for `${IFS}`, `${@}` and slices such as `${x:0:5}`, and `$(...)` and backtick pairs only when they hold a command with arguments or a known command, so `export PATH=${HOME}/bin`, `${amount}`, `$(CC) $(CFLAGS)`, `$('#submit-button')`, a word between backticks and a FreeMarker `${p.name}` are clean while `$(id)`, `` `id` ``, `${IFS}cat`, `' UNION SELECT` and `1 and sleep(0.5)` are flagged. Every Log4j lookup stays flagged as `cmd_injection`: `${jndi:...}` with any protocol, the lookup prefixes `env`, `sys`, `ctx`, `java`, `bundle`, `main`, `date`, `base64`, `lower`, `upper`, `marker`, `sd`, `map`, `k8s`, `docker`, `spring`, `web` and `log4j` (`${env:AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY}`), and the nested obfuscations of `jndi` (`${${::-j}${::-n}di:ldap://x/a}`, `${${env:NaN:-j}ndi:...}`), on every surface and in an excluded header. A value that was flagged only through these rows is no longer blocked, logged as a threat, counted as a strike or reported in a `penetration_attempt` event; there is no setting to bring the old matching back.
- **`${7*7}` is reported as `template` only, no longer as `cmd_injection` and `template`** (release note, behavior change). The value is still flagged, but a plain `${...}` expression is not a shell substitution any more (see the previous entry), so the `cmd_injection` label is gone from `${7*7}` and the same family of arithmetic and call expressions. Anything keyed on the category sees `template` alone: a `threat_ban_config` entry for `cmd_injection` no longer counts it, and `excluded_detection_params={"q": {"template"}}` (or the same mapping for body fields) now clears `${7*7}` on that parameter, where excluding `template` alone left it flagged through `cmd_injection`. If you excluded `cmd_injection` for such a parameter to let `${...}` through, exclude `template` as well, otherwise the value is flagged as `template`.
- **A verdict can carry more categories than in 4.4.0, and each one is excluded and counted on its own** (release note, behavior change). A request that 4.4.0 flagged under one category is now flagged under every category whose row matches. Absolute filesystem paths as a value (`/var/www/.env`, `/srv/app/settings.py`, `/usr/bin/env bash -c id`) add `dir_traversal`; named webshell, upload-directory, PHPUnit and `/language/...` probes and config-named files (`/c99.php`, `/uploads/shell.php`, `/appsettings.json`) add `recon` or `sensitive_file` next to the other; a command, a backtick pair, `eval(`, `system(`, `exec(` or `sh -c` in the URL path adds `cmd_injection` next to `ssrf`, `deserialization`, `file_inclusion`, `template` or `dir_traversal`; and an SQL injection that carries a comment (`UNION ALL SELECT @@VERSION,USER(),SLEEP(5),BENCHMARK(1000000,MD5('A'))--`) adds a semantic `suspicious` entry to `sqli` because the semantic score is now taken on the view that keeps the SQL comments as well as on the view that drops them, and the higher of the two is used. Path rule v2 (decision D-71) also flags absolute paths that 4.4.0 passed, such as `/usr/local/bin/python` and `C:\Users\bob\file.txt`, as `dir_traversal`; that is a documented 5.0.0 change. For the other values here the flagged requests do not change, and what changes is that `enabled_detection_categories`, the mapping form of `excluded_detection_params` and `excluded_detection_body_fields`, `threat_ban_config` and the per-category counts of events see the added labels: to clear `/appsettings.json` on one parameter, exclude both `recon` and `sensitive_file` (excluding only `recon` leaves it flagged as `sensitive_file`), and a `threat_ban_config` entry for `dir_traversal`, `recon` or `cmd_injection` can now apply to a request it did not apply to before.
- **A request the IP lists deny reads its ban and records its strike in one Redis round trip** (release notes). With `ban_on_hostile_signals` on, a client that the block list, the allow list or a route's IP lists denies cost two round trips per blocked request (the ban read, then the strike). One script now does both when the client is not banned, so the cost per blocked request is one round trip, as in 4.4.0. The keys, counters, bans and events are unchanged. A client another worker bans between the two steps no longer records a strike (the two calls were not atomic before): this is intended and stricter under races. Requests blocked by the other checks (user agent, referrer, authentication, required headers, cloud provider, custom validators, spoofing) still make two.
- **A ban read waits for the first check that needs it** (release notes). When the local ban lookup has no answer and the IP lists allow the client, the IP check no longer reads the Redis ban keys on the spot. The read rides the round trip of the first hostile denial of a later check (cloud provider, user agent, the spoofing signal), the rate-limit script (ban first: a banned client increments no counter and is never answered with a 429), or a plain read before any other terminal response (an allowed CORS preflight included), before the first check that cannot wait, or at the end of the pipeline. A banned client gets the same response, log line and events as before, and none of the later check's own side effects. A clean rate-limited request makes one Redis round trip instead of two, a user-agent-blocked request one instead of two, a spoofing request one instead of three.
