Requests blocked by required headers, authentication, referrer and custom validators still make two, because their strike comes after code that can run user logic.
- **Throttle rules refuse under the released adapters, route and global** (release note, behavior change). Before, a usage `throttle` rule (`BehaviorRule` or a global `behavior_rules` entry) only refused under an adapter that ran the usage rules itself and returned their answer; the released fastapi-guard, flaskapi-guard, djapi-guard and tornadoapi-guard call `process_usage_rules` as a bare statement and drop its refusal, so a throttle rule recorded its trips and never answered. The engine now runs the usage rules (route and global) as the last step of `SecurityCheckPipeline.execute`, after every blocking check, and a tripped throttle answers `429 Too many requests` with `Retry-After`, whichever adapter version wraps it. A request that a ban, a rate limit or a detection already refused does not reach the step. Each request records its hit once: the adapter's own later `process_usage_rules` call returns the stored result and records nothing. In passive mode the trip is logged and the request is admitted. With a released adapter, global `ban` and `log` usage rules also record before the handler runs instead of after the response (a request whose handler fails is still counted, as before). If your clients began to receive `429` after the upgrade, a throttle rule you configured is now doing what it says; raise its threshold or window to restore the old traffic.
- **`on_block` fires for behavior throttle refusals** (release note, behavior change). The hook now runs once per throttle refusal (`status_code` 429) and, in passive mode, once per request that would have been refused (`status_code=None`), with `check_name` `behavior_throttle`. Before it never fired for a throttle trip, against its documented once-per-block-decision contract. The adapter's later marked `process_usage_rules` call fires nothing, so one request gives one call.
- **An excluded health path answers 503 in emergency mode unless it is in `emergency_exempt_paths`** (release note, behavior change). Emergency mode now also covers `exclude_paths` entries: with `emergency_mode=True`, a client outside `emergency_whitelist` gets `503 Service temporarily unavailable` with `Retry-After: 60` on an excluded `/health`, `/ping` or `/docs` as on any other path (in passive mode the block is logged and the request admitted). A probe path that must stay reachable during a lockdown goes in the new `emergency_exempt_paths` list (a path and its subtree, never `/`); loopback is not exempted automatically. When `emergency_mode` is on and an `exclude_paths` entry looks like a probe path (health, ping, live, ready, status and the like) and is not exempted, a startup WARNING names it. A load balancer or orchestrator probe that hit an excluded path now fails its check during emergency mode unless that path is listed.
- **`max_request_size(0)` refuses any request body with 413; use `None` for no limit** (release note, behavior change). `0` used to skip the size check, so a route decorated `max_request_size(0)` accepted any body. It now refuses every request that carries a body byte, while a request with `Content-Length: 0` or no body passes; chunked and length-less bodies are counted as they are read. Applying `max_request_size(0)` logs one WARNING per route ("0 now refuses any request body; use None for no limit"), and a negative size raises `ValueError` when the decorator is applied. If you wrote `0` to mean unlimited, you carried over nginx's `client_max_body_size 0` convention, where `0` disables the check; here `0` means no body allowed, so those requests now get `413`. Remove the decorator or pass `None` for no limit.
- **An HTTPS redirect for a request that is not a GET or HEAD answers `308`, not `301`** (release note, behavior change). `enforce_https` and `@require_https` redirect a POST, PUT, PATCH or DELETE with `308 Permanent Redirect`, which keeps the method and the body, so the client repeats the same request over HTTPS. GET and HEAD keep `301`. A client or test that expected `301` for an unsafe method sees `308`.
- **A route's `block_clouds` adds to the global `block_cloud_providers` instead of replacing it** (release note, behavior change). On a route that set `block_clouds`, v4.4.0 checked only the route's providers and the global list did not apply. The route now blocks the union of its own providers and the global ones, and the ranges of the route's providers are loaded at startup and on refresh, so they block from the first request. A route cannot narrow the global list: use `bypass(["clouds"])` to skip the cloud check on one route.
- **Dynamic rules add to the static lists, a malformed rule is rejected whole and a platform default is ignored** (release note, behavior change). A dynamic `blocked_countries`, `blocked_cloud_providers` or `blocked_user_agents` is added to your static list and never removes or replaces it (v4.4.0 replaced the country, cloud and user-agent lists with the rule's), a dynamic `whitelist_countries` replaces the static allow-list only when it is non-empty, and revoking the rule restores exactly the static part. A dynamic `ip_whitelist` entry joins `exempt_ips` while its rule is active: it skips rate limiting and the user-agent and cloud checks and is unbanned when the rule applies, detection still blocks its individual requests, it is never added to the access allow-list, and an entry listed in both `ip_blacklist` and `ip_whitelist` is whitelisted and reported in the applied event, which also lists every skipped entry. A rule version with a malformed value (a limit below 1, an IP or CIDR that does not parse, a bad country code, an unknown cloud provider, an `endpoint_rate_limits` key with a dot segment, an empty segment, a backslash or an encoded dot or separator) is rejected as a whole: the engine keeps the last-known rules, logs one WARNING and sends a `dynamic_rule_updated` event with `action_taken="rules_rejected"` and the problems in `metadata.rejected`. A version 0 answer reverts the applied rule, a rule flagged `is_platform_default` is ignored with one WARNING, and the swap from one version to the next is applied in one step. No switch brings the replace behavior back: remove the static entries a rule should replace.
- **Global behavior rules count per client across every endpoint** (release note, behavior change). A usage, frequency or return-pattern rule in `global_behavior_rules` now counts every request or response that passes through the guard, routed or not, in one counter per client and rule; v4.4.0 counted per endpoint, so a global `status:401` ban rule fires sooner across endpoints. A route-level rule stays per endpoint. The hashed key of a global rule changed, so old and new instances count in separate buckets during a rolling upgrade.
- **Country and cloud denials send their own events next to `ip_blocked`, and `cloud_blocked` carries a `rule_type`** (release note, behavior change). A request denied by a country rule sends `country_blocked` (`rule_type` `country_blacklist` or `country_whitelist`) as well as `ip_blocked`; v4.4.0 sent no `country_blocked` on a request. A request denied by the global cloud list sends `ip_blocked` with the provider and network next to `cloud_blocked`, and a route `block_clouds` denial sends `cloud_blocked` and `decorator_violation` and no `ip_blocked`. Every `cloud_blocked` carries `rule_type="cloud_blacklist"`. A consumer that counts denials by event type counts a global country or cloud denial twice unless it pairs the events, as the Guard Core SaaS does.
- **A direct `unban_ip` call reports `manual_unban`** (release note, behavior change). `unban_ip(ip, reason=...)` takes a `reason` keyword. A direct call sends the `ip_unbanned` event with `reason="manual_unban"`, where v4.4.0 sent `dynamic_rule_whitelist` for every unban; the dynamic-rule whitelist path keeps `dynamic_rule_whitelist` and a rule revocation sends `dynamic_rule_revoked`. A dashboard or alert keyed on the old reason for manual unbans needs the new value.
- **A path with a dot segment, an empty segment, a backslash or an encoded dot or separator is never excluded, and such `exclude_paths` entries and `endpoint_rate_limits` keys are misconfigurations** (release note, behavior change). The adapters pass the path the framework routes on and the engine no longer decodes or collapses it again to decide `exclude_paths`, so `//health`, `/health/../admin` and `/%2e%2e/health` match no exclusion where v4.4.0 collapsed them first, while detection still scans a collapsed, dot-resolved copy (`//.env` is flagged like `/.env`). An `exclude_paths` entry or an `endpoint_rate_limits` key with those shapes, in any decoding round, is reported through `fail_on_misconfiguration` (a startup failure by default) and a dynamic rule carrying one is rejected. Each adapter resolves an `endpoint_rate_limits` key through its own router, so a key counts the requests that reach the same handler, and an `exclude_paths` entry that matches no registered route logs one startup WARNING that asks you to check it for a typo. Write the plain path.
- **Security header defaults: the app's own header wins, `Cross-Origin-Embedder-Policy` is not sent and `Cross-Origin-Resource-Policy` is `same-site`** (release note, behavior change). A default header is added only when the application did not set that header (compared without case); a value you configured (`custom`, `csp`, `hsts`, a changed default) still replaces the application's. `Cross-Origin-Embedder-Policy` is no longer sent unless you set `cross_origin_embedder_policy` (v4.4.0 sent `require-corp`), and `Cross-Origin-Resource-Policy` defaults to `same-site` (v4.4.0 sent `same-origin`). A partial `security_headers` dict merges over the defaults key by key, `None`, `{}` and `{"enabled": False}` switch every header off, a value of `None` switches that one header off, and an unknown key is reported through `fail_on_misconfiguration`. Restore the old headers: `security_headers={"cross_origin_embedder_policy": "require-corp", "cross_origin_resource_policy": "same-origin"}`.
- **A malformed `X-Forwarded-For` entry from a trusted proxy answers `400`, and the untrusted spoofing event is sampled** (release note, behavior change). When the entry that `trusted_proxy_depth` selects is empty or not an IP address, the request is attributed to its own identity `malformed-xff@<proxy>` instead of the proxy's address, which v4.4.0 fell back to. Under `fail_secure=True` it answers `400` with a log line and a `suspicious_request` event (`action_taken="malformed_forwarded_for"`); with it off the request goes on under that identity, which is rate limited and bannable like any client and passes only when no `whitelist` or `whitelist_countries` is set. The warning for it is logged once a minute per peer. The `suspicious_request` event with `action_taken="spoofing_detected"` that an untrusted peer's `X-Forwarded-For` sends is sampled per peer the same way and carries `suppressed_count` in its metadata, so a flood from one peer sends one event a minute, not one per request.
- **The GeoIP database load costs about 0.2 ms of CPU more, once per process** (release note, accepted cost). Every load path (startup, `initialize_redis`, the lazy first request and the stale-file refresh) now goes through one shared single-flight load, so the first requests of a cold start wait for that one load instead of each starting their own, a request that cancels does not cancel it, and a load that raises answers like v4.4.0 (`500` under `fail_secure`, a pass with it off). Measured against v4.4.0, startup `initialize()` costs 0.16 to 0.28 ms CPU more in each tree (async 0.40 against 0.21 to 0.24 ms, sync 0.47 against 0.19 to 0.21 ms), and a cold load inside the first request costs 0.2 to 0.3 ms more. The cost per request after the load is unchanged.
- **A JSON document inside a JSON string field, a body sent under a non-JSON content type and a deeply nested value are scanned, and the file lists of known webhooks are not flagged** (release note, behavior change). A stringified JSON nested two or more levels deep with an escaped payload passed in a JSON body in 4.4.0, and is now flagged like the same value in a query. Every scanned value goes through the embedded JSON walk behind a cheap prefilter, and a value nested deeper than one fixed engine limit (512, below the lowest parser limit on Python 3.10 to 3.14) has its escaped string literals decoded by a flat lexer under the same budgets, so every supported Python gives the same verdict. A body sent as `text/plain` or `application/octet-stream` that a framework would still parse as JSON is walked only through the string literals that hold a backslash, so a flat body costs what it did. A plain path in a push change list, a review comment (a `path` with `diff_hunk` or `position`), a pull request file (a `filename` with `patch` or `status`) or a Sentry stack frame (an `abs_path` with `lineno` or `function`) is not a sensitive file access, while traversal, trick suffixes, query strings and the same values outside those shapes stay flagged. Bare script names (`index.php`, `config.inc.php`, `app.rb`, `run.sh`, `q.sql`) in a query value or a JSON body field are flagged again as in v4.4.0, and an undeclared `text/plain` body stays unflagged for these names. Anything else that is a webhook's file list and is flagged goes through `detection_exclusion` on that route.
- **The named sensitive-file rows take the trick suffixes of the source-extension row, and a list of credential files is flagged as `sensitive_file`** (release note, behavior change, LEDGER 6.95 and 6.96). v4.4.0 flagged `.env`, `.git/config` and `.htpasswd` only as a plain name: `.env;.jpg`, `.env.`, `.git/config;.jpg` and `.htpasswd;.jpg` passed on the query, in JSON and on the URL path. Every named row now ends in the same suffix group as the source-extension row: `;` parameters, trailing dots, `::$DATA`, a NUL tail (`%00`, `\u0000`, `\x00` or the deleted control character) and an image extension. New rows flag the credential files an attacker reads by name: the private keys under `.ssh/` (`id_rsa`, `id_dsa`, `id_ecdsa`, `id_ed25519` and the `_sk` forms) and `authorized_keys`, `.aws/credentials`, `.docker/config.json`, `.kube/config`, the Azure, GitHub CLI, gcloud, Terraform, Maven, Gradle, Cargo, gem and Composer credential files, and by name `.git-credentials`, `.netrc`, `.pgpass`, `.pypirc`, `.vault-token`, `.s3cfg`, `.boto`, `terraform.tfstate` and the shell histories. The credential names join the `.env` row, so a request costs no additional regex call. A bare private key name counts only as a URL path segment, and a value that is only a home-relative reference to one of these files (`~/.aws/credentials`, a setting that names where the file lives) stays clean, as in v4.4.0.

### Fixed

- **One megabyte of hex digits, percent escapes or `../` no longer holds 50 to 150 MB of heap in the decoders and the path-traversal view (LEDGER 6.480).** Python's `re` keeps a stack frame of about 140 bytes for every iteration of a repeated group, and a list of match objects costs about 180 bytes each. `decode_hex_text_runs` matched `(?:[0-9a-fA-F]{2}){6,}`, `decode_overlong_utf8_percent_runs` matched `(?:%[0-9a-fA-F]{2})+`, the lost-byte repair ran a Python function for every `../` segment of the text, and the decoded-view traversal check listed every `../` match of the body. A 1 MiB percent-encoded multipart upload (its text ends in a long run of `a` padding, which is a run of hex digits) held 50.5 MB of Python heap and 64 of them in flight peaked at 2,954 to 3,165 MB RSS, one 1 MiB run of hex digits held 75 MB, one of `%41` escapes 48 MB and one of `../` 65 MB, which was above v4.4.0 for the `../` body (55 MB). The hex pattern is now `[0-9a-fA-F]{12,}` with an even-length check (the same runs decode), the percent runs are matched in chunks of 1024 escapes that are joined before they are decoded (the same decoding, with `bytes.fromhex` in place of a per-byte generator), the lost-byte repair is one substitution without a callback, and the traversal check counts `../` and `..\` with `str.count` and takes the first match. `SusPatternsManager.detect` on 26 shapes of 1 MiB now holds no more than v4.4.0 on every shape (the hex run 4.8 MB against 151.6 MB at v4.4.0 and 74.6 MB before, `%41` 16.5 against 74.8 and 48.5, `../` 50.9 against 55.1 and 64.9), and one 1 MiB percent-encoded upload holds 8.5 MB of heap instead of 50.5 (v4.4.0: 31.7 MB at its smaller scan window, 149 MB when it scans the same 1 MiB). With 64 of them in flight the peak RSS is 482 to 872 MB per adapter, against 6.8 to 7.0 GB for v4.4.0 on the async reader and on Django when it scans the same bytes; the multipart table of `docs/configuration/detection-tuning.md` carries every adapter and body, and `perf_gate/multipart_cells.py` reproduces a cell. Tests keep a 6 MB bound on each decoder and an 8 MB bound on the traversal check for a megabyte run, and compare every rewritten pattern with the pattern it replaced on thousands of generated strings.
- **64 Django uploads of a 1 MiB binary file no longer peak above v4.4.0 (LEDGER 6.499).** The sync tree runs the scan on the request thread, so 64 uploads in flight all sat in the multipart parse at once and each held the decoded body (3.9 MB, because one character above U+FFFF makes Python store every character in four bytes), the parsed payload (3.9 MB) and the adapter's copy of the body: 757 to 774 MB of RSS against 529 to 573 MB at v4.4.0, whose four-worker regex pool kept all but four of them out of the parse. A multipart body is now decoded in 8 KiB steps while the parser reads it, so the whole decoded body never exists next to the parsed payload (`_HeaderThenBody` takes bytes and decodes them with the same UTF-8 and `surrogateescape` rules, and the form, JSON and blob scans decode where they did before), and at most four multipart parses run at once in a process, the same four that v4.4.0's pool allowed (a slot is returned when a parse fails and a forked child starts with all four free). Nothing is scanned less and nothing is refused: a fifth upload waits for a slot. One 1 MiB binary upload holds 10.0 MB of heap instead of 13.8 MB (v4.4.0: 21.7 MB), 64 in flight peak at 282 to 284 MB of RSS (v4.4.0: 529 to 573 MB, three alternating runs each) and cost 0.109 s of CPU per MB (v4.4.0: 0.145 to 0.168). Calls per request are unchanged (allowed GET 30,397, JSON 54,173, multipart 83,371) and detection recall against the 10,047 corpus keys is unchanged. Tests bound the live heap when the parts are scanned (3 MiB) and the peak of one upload (8 MiB), compare the bytes reader with the decoded string on generated bodies, and pin the slot count, its release on failure and the fork reset.

- **A regex-worker queue wait answers 503 with `Retry-After`, and a scan that only waited for the GIL is never a threat (LEDGER 6.481, D-230).** Under 64 uploads in flight on Flask and Django 30 of 640 benign 1 MiB uploads were refused at 31454662: 18 were queue-wait expiries answered `400 Request body could not be read`, and 12 were `400 Suspicious activity detected` with an `uncategorized` strike, from started windowed-row jobs that outlived the 2 s wall bound while using 10.8 to 15.5 ms of worker CPU (the worker was starved of the GIL, not busy). The six windowed built-in rows (`cmd_injection` shell `-c`, the two LDAP null-byte rows, quote splice, pickle global and XXE public DTD) now run on the request thread like every other built-in row, judged by the wall trip then the thread's own CPU (each finder is linear: 1 MiB and 10 MiB measured with `python -m perf_gate.windowed_finders_linear`, growth for ten times the input 9.0 to 13.2 on every row that costs more than 5 ms, worst 0.50 s of CPU at 1 MiB). A started custom scan is judged by its worker's CPU when it finishes and cut only at `detection_max_scan_seconds` of wall time (it was cut at `detection_compiler_timeout` of wall time). A scan that gets no worker within `detection_compiler_timeout` raises `RegexQueueWaitError`, a subclass of `BodyReadFailedError` (every existing handler still catches it), and the request answers `503` with `Retry-After` set to `ceil(detection_compiler_timeout)`, no strike and no suspicious or ban count, one `suspicious_request` event and a log line that names the wait and the surface (`Request scan waited for a regex worker for IP: <ip> (surface: query)`); `DetectionResult.body_read_error` is `"queue_wait"` with `queue_wait_seconds` and `queue_wait_surface`. A real body read failure keeps its `400`, its log line and its event word for word. `fail_secure=False` and passive mode pass with the event as before, and the expiry still counts toward replacing a pool held by stuck workers. `custom_error_responses[503]` replaces the message. Three alternating runs of 64 uploads in flight per arm on Flask and Django (1 MiB text and percent-encoded bodies) refused 0 of 768 at this head and 32 of 768 at 31454662 (15 queue waits and 17 threat verdicts with a strike).

- **`SecurityConfig().model_dump_json()` and `SecurityConfig.model_json_schema()` work for a default config and for a config with every field set (LEDGER 6.476).** Both raised at v4.4.0 and at the 5.0.0 head: `PydanticSerializationError` on the `threat_ban_config` mapping, and `PydanticInvalidForJsonSchema` on it and on the handler and callback fields. `threat_ban_config` now serializes as a mapping of category to its `threshold` and `duration`. `geo_ip_handler`, `cloud_ip_store`, `custom_request_check`, `custom_response_modifier`, `auth_verifier`, `on_error` and `on_block` serialize as the dotted module and qualified name of the object (`null` when unset), so a secret a handler holds never reaches the JSON, and the JSON schema describes each of them as a string or `null`. `model_dump()` still returns the live objects, validation and the field types are unchanged, and the first `SecurityConfig()` costs the same on both trees, with logging on and off (CPU time of a cold process, interleaved with an A/A arm: the difference to the head is inside the spread of the A/A pair). `docs/configuration/security-config.md` shows the calls.

- **Every performance number the pages state is measured on 5.0.0, with the command that reproduces it (LEDGER 6.478).** `perf_gate/detection_components.py` (`latency`, `tuning` and `memory`, on the async engine and with `--tree sync` on the sync one), `perf_gate/ban_store_memory.py` and `perf_gate/multipart_cells.py` measure CPU time per call (interleaved with an A/A arm), heap and RSS per step, the memory of a full ban store and the cost of 64 uploads in flight, and the pages quote their output. The cold import of `import guard_core` is about 25 ms and `import guard_core.models` about 0.3 s (the pages said roughly 2 ms), a full ban store is about 23 MiB (the pages said 25), and the multipart table of `docs/configuration/detection-tuning.md` is re-measured per adapter and per body, which adds the percent-encoded body: 50.5 to 52.6 MB of heap per request and 2,954 to 3,165 MB of RSS with 64 in flight.

- **Every shipped Markdown example and stated default matches the 5.0.0 API, and a test keeps it true (LEDGER 6.446, 6.458).** `tests/test_markdown_python_blocks.py` parses every Python block of the README, `docs/`, the specs and the packaged skill files, runs the blocks of each file one after another in a sandbox with no network, and checks every guard-core import, call, keyword and attribute against the installed objects. It also compares every default a table or bullet states with `SecurityConfig`, checks the documented signature listings, and checks the documented telemetry constants against `EVENT_TYPE_VALUES`, `METRIC_TYPE_VALUES` and `CHECK_NAME_VALUES`. It failed on the old pages, and the pages now show what 5.0.0 accepts: the pipeline catalogue is 18 checks (`cors_preflight` sits between `user_agent` and `rate_limit`, and a default `SecurityConfig()` builds five checks), the detection engine constructors list every parameter (`validation_cache`, `redaction_names`, `max_full_scan_bytes`, `min_samples_for_anomaly`), the preprocessor decodes up to 16 passes of overlong UTF-8, percent, HTML entity, `%u`, hex, octal, UTF-7, LDAP hex, Unicode escape and base64 layers, `slow_pattern_threshold` is bounded by 1.0, the telemetry event list has the 44 values of `EVENT_TYPE_VALUES` (`behavioral_violation`), `SemanticAnalyzer.analyze` returns a `SemanticAnalysis`, a custom pattern that the ReDoS validator rejects is replaced by one it accepts, signature listings are valid Python, and the skill files no longer show shell commands as Python or a check count of 17. `scripts/unasync.py` skips the new test, which runs against the async and sync trees alike.

- **The ReDoS validator refuses a complete character class repeated to a large bound on every Python, not only on 3.10 to 3.13 (LEDGER 6.470).** Python 3.14 compiles a class that holds every character, such as `[\s\S]`, into its any-character opcode and skips a fixed repeat of it in one step, so `[\s\S]{4095}[^\u2603]` cost about a quarter of a millisecond at 32000 characters there and was accepted (v4.4.0 accepted it too), while 3.10 to 3.13 take about a second and refuse it. The timing children now compile the way the older engines do, so the verdict is the same on 3.10 to 3.14 for `[\s\S]`, `[\d\D]` and `[\w\W]` at every bound; `(?s:.){4095}[^\u2603]`, which every engine skips in one step, stays accepted.
- **Validating a pattern costs the parent process no more CPU than in 4.4.0 (LEDGER 6.471).** The validator built every candidate probe set and kept all of them in memory before timing the first one (hundreds of megabytes of probe strings for one pattern, about 770 MB of resident memory after a handful of patterns), and every probe child it then started copied that address space, so the parent spent 3 to 5 CPU seconds on a pattern whose children cost 1. It now hashes each candidate to find the unique sets and the stride sample, keeps only their positions, and builds each sampled probe set when it is about to be timed; the probes, their order and the sampling are unchanged. The Unicode category tables (`\d`, `\s`, `\w` and their negations) are read from one regex scan of the code points instead of a Python call per code point, and the macOS work counter loads `libproc` by name instead of through `ctypes.util`, which cost each probe child about 7 ms of CPU. No verdict changes.
- **A pattern validation spawns its timing children with less parent CPU (LEDGER 6.471, 6.472).** Each child's payload went through a fresh temporary file and its heartbeats through a second one, about 0.13 ms of parent CPU each, so adding a custom pattern cost more parent CPU than in 4.4.0 (53.3 ms for `add_pattern` of 20 patterns against 48.6 ms). A payload of at most 4096 bytes now goes through a pipe written in full before the first wait (a larger one still goes through a file, for the Python 3.10 to 3.13 retry reason above), and each thread keeps one heartbeat file that it empties before every child. The parent CPU of one spawn is 1.21 ms against 1.19 ms for a plain `subprocess.run` and 1.65 ms before, and no verdict changes.
- **A test string's verdict in the pattern safety probe, and the safety-gates generator's margin check for it, follow the retired-instruction count where the host reports one (LEDGER 6.472).** The `test_strings` path refused a string whose search took more than 0.05 s of CPU, a clock that a loaded host or an efficiency core stretches, while the reach probes already read the count. The probe child now compares the counted work (the same unit as the reach probes) with that threshold on macOS and keeps the CPU-time reading elsewhere, with the same refusal text. `safety_test_strings_probe_string_timeout_01` was not a load flake: its string costs about 0.13 s of CPU on Python 3.14 against 0.30 s on 3.10, so the generator's 5x margin over the threshold failed on every 3.14 run; where the count exists the generator now needs a 2x margin on the counted cost (3.14: 3.2x, 3.10: 6.0x) and keeps 5x on a CPU-time reading.
- **A pattern validation no longer fails when its timing child starts late on Python 3.10 to 3.13 (LEDGER 6.488).** The validator hands each timing child its probes through a pipe, and `communicate()` on those Pythons does not resume writing the input after its first timeout, so a child that began more than half a second late (a loaded host, background priority) never received the rest of a payload larger than the pipe buffer, showed no CPU progress and was killed as stalled: a safe pattern was refused with the killable-subprocess timeout reason, the stray pipe surfaced as a `ResourceWarning`, and the safety-gates generator lost a case. The payload now goes in through a file the child reads as its stdin. Verdicts do not change.
- **A probe child that waits for a CPU slice, or has just exited, is no longer killed as stalled, and a reach-probe child's CPU limit follows the host load factor (LEDGER 6.488).** The stall window killed any child whose heartbeat count had not moved for 30 s of wall time. The parent now reads the child's process state first (`/proc/<pid>/stat`, else `ps`) and keeps waiting while it is runnable (a starved host) or already exited (its result is waiting to be collected); a blocked, stopped or unreadable child is still killed, and a runaway still dies at its own CPU limit. The budget of a validation was already stretched by the measured load factor, but each child was killed at the unscaled 40 s of CPU (2.5 s for a single probe): a worst-case row costs 33 s of CPU on an efficiency core (load factor about 3) against 16 s on a performance core, so it came within 20 percent of the kill and left its class with the killable-subprocess timeout reason. The limit now carries the load factor, never below the idle default and never past the remaining budget. No verdict changes on an idle host.
- **Where only signal polls are counted, a row whose load-normalized CPU time is over the validator budget is refused whatever the count reads, as in 4.4.0 (LEDGER 6.473, 6.504, behavior change from earlier 5.0.0 builds).** A row mixing work the matcher polls on with a scan inside one opcode (which the count cannot see) could read under the budget by count while its CPU time was above it. Earlier 5.0.0 builds let the count decide up to 2 times the budget in CPU time, and then up to 1.5 times; a custom pattern in that range, for example a `blocked_user_agents` entry (the configuration checks it when it is built, `_validate_blocked_user_agents_value`), was accepted by those builds and is refused again, as the CPU-time verdict of 4.4.0 refused it. Measured on macOS with the instruction counter switched off, such rows (for example `<![CDATA[` repeated 500 times followed by 8000 `a` against `<!\[CDATA\[.*?\]\]>|a*b`) read 0.08 to 0.97 of the budget by count and 1.07 to 1.78 by CPU time, and every one of them is refused; the rows that read under the budget by CPU time stay accepted. The worst load-normalized best sample of any safe row (the 4.4.0 CDATA row at cap 10000) is 0.98 of the budget on Python 3.10 and 0.97 on 3.14, so the margin is 2 percent, the margin the CPU-time verdict of 4.4.0 had. A host with no counter still cannot tell a row at 1.0 to 1.02 times the budget from one at 0.98 by CPU time, because a CPU reading normalized by a reference scan is no finer than that; an instruction counter that sees inside one opcode would close it, and Windows has none.
- **The `cms_probing` backup-extension row costs less to match (LEDGER 6.128).** The path-only row for `.bak`, `.backup`, `.old`, `.orig`, `.save`, `.swp`, `.swo`, `.tmp` and `.temp` names now factors `bak|backup` as `b(?:ak|ackup)`, which accepts exactly the same names and takes about 11 percent less matching work (measured on 3.10: 0.536 to 0.479 of the validator budget, on 3.14: 0.418 to 0.369), so the row sits under half the budget on both Pythons. No conformance case changes.
- **Where no instruction counter exists (Linux, other Unix), the ReDoS validator's reach-probe verdict counts CPython's signal polls beside the CPU time (LEDGER 6.473, 6.472).** Linux refuses `perf_event_open` to a container and to `perf_event_paranoid` above 2, so the verdict there stayed a measured time, and in a `python:3.14-slim` container on an idle host the lowest CDATA mirror cap was accepted once at 0.0492 s against the 0.05 s budget. The probe child now counts the signal polls CPython's matcher makes (one per few thousand opcode dispatches, the same number on Linux and macOS: 1605 for the 3.10 cap 13000 mirror, 1954 for the 3.14 cap 12000 mirror) with a re-arming SIGINT and a wakeup pipe, one unit for every Python from 3.10 to 3.14. The count decides while the load-normalized CPU time stays within 3 times of it and 2 times of the budget; work the count cannot see (a scan inside one opcode) is still decided by the CPU time. In `python:3.10-slim` and `python:3.14-slim` containers, idle, beside 4 busy loops and beside 24, the lowest mirror caps were refused 60 of 60 times per Python and cap (the CPU-time verdict accepted 3.14 cap 12000 once), the safe cap 10000 was accepted 60 of 60, no probe row gave a wrong verdict, and every counted row read the same cost in all three cells. Windows keeps the CPU-time verdict. The safety-gates generator's check of `safety_test_strings_probe_string_timeout_01` needs 2 times over the threshold and under the kill for a CPU-time reading, because the case costs 0.12 to 0.30 s depending on the Python and the host, a spread no single string fits between 5 times on both sides; it passes on both Pythons in the same cells.
- **An allowed in-memory rate-limit check costs no more CPU than in 4.4.0 (LEDGER 6.427).** The multi-tier verdict machinery made one allowed `check_rate_limit` build a tier, a tier list and a verdict and test `redis_is_configured` before it looked at the window: 15 Python and C calls and 1.05 us of CPU against 8 calls and 0.49 us in 4.4.0. A check with no Redis handler now slides the one window directly (the same key, window, limit and Retry-After as before) and builds the tier and the verdict only for a blocked request; the multi-tier pipeline path tests the handler before `redis_is_configured`. No behavior changes.
- **With `passive_mode=True` a request whose client has no address is admitted and logged, not refused with 403 (LEDGER 6.399, D-188, behavior change).** A connection with no client address at all (no trusted-proxy header to recover one) was answered 403 under `fail_secure=True` even in passive mode, which never blocks. In passive mode it now logs the one-time warning (which says the request is admitted and logged with identity `unknown`), fires `on_block` with `passive_mode=True` as every passive block does, and the pipeline runs with identity `unknown`. Without passive mode the 403 stays.
- **The `enable_dynamic_rules` description and the configuration page say what a rule does to each setting (LEDGER 6.391).** Both said a rule replaces `blocked_countries`, `block_cloud_providers` and `blocked_user_agents`; the code adds a rule's entries to the configured ones and never removes a static entry (D-22 6.04). They now say which settings a rule adds to (`blocked_countries`, `block_cloud_providers`, `blocked_user_agents`), which it merges per endpoint (`endpoint_rate_limits`, D-219) and which it replaces while it is active, and a test pins the text.
- **A dynamic rule's `endpoint_rate_limits` merges per endpoint with the static map (LEDGER 6.389, D-219, behavior change).** The rule's map replaced the static one, which silently dropped every static limit on an endpoint the rule does not name and so widened access the operator never asked to widen. While a rule is active the effective map is `{**static_snapshot, **rule_map}`, computed from the static snapshot on every apply (an older rule's keys never pile up): the rule's value wins on the endpoints it names, a static limit on any other endpoint keeps applying, and expiry or revoke restores the static map.
- **A behavior throttle also admits when Redis fails open, and an AWS entry with no `ip_prefix` no longer drops the whole AWS set (LEDGER 6.370, 6.375).** The throttle's Redis-error path ignored `redis_fail_open` (the pipeline admits on it); it now admits with the error still logged. An AWS range entry without an `ip_prefix` or `service` key raised `KeyError` and emptied the provider; it is now skipped and counted with the invalid prefixes.
- **`@block_countries([])` and `@block_clouds([])` report the empty list (LEDGER 6.390).** They were accepted silently where `@allow_countries([])` and `@require_ip(whitelist=[])` report the empty list through the misconfiguration check. An empty list blocks nothing, so `@block_countries([])` and `@block_clouds([])` now raise `ValueError` with `fail_on_misconfiguration=True` and log one WARNING with it off; `@block_clouds()` with no argument still blocks every provider.
- **Undoing a discarded `custom_response_modifier` call restores every value of a multi-valued header (LEDGER 6.386).** The undo log noted `real[key]`, one value, so after the first call of a replayed modifier overwrote or deleted `Set-Cookie`, the undo restored one value of N (`['a=1', 'b=2']` became `['a=1']`). Headers that can hold several values of a name (Starlette's `MutableHeaders`) now note a full snapshot before a set or a delete and are restored from it.
- **A response `Content-Length` with a non-ASCII digit no longer raises out of the body gate (LEDGER 6.378).** `str.isdigit()` is true for `²` and other digits that `int()` rejects, so a response header such as `Content-Length: ²` (only the app's own response can carry one) raised `ValueError` out of `declared_content_length` and `body_gate_refusal`. Only ASCII digits are a length now; anything else is "declares no Content-Length", as before for a letter. The LDAP IPv4 octet check reads digits the same way.
- **Rule attribution matches the normalized country and the CIDR ranges of a dynamic rule (LEDGER 6.377).** The `guard.rule.id` enrichment compared an event's country and IP with the rule's raw lists, so a rule that blocks `us` was not attributed for an event with country `US`, and an event from inside a banned `203.0.113.0/24` was not attributed. The country is compared upper-cased on both sides and the address is matched against the list entries and ranges as the engine matches them.
- **`${7*7}` is cmd_injection again, as in 4.4.0 (LEDGER 6.376).** A `${...}` whose content is only digits and arithmetic operators (`${7*7}`, `${1+1}`) was reported as `[cmd_injection, template]` by 4.4.0 and as `[template]` alone since the dollar-substitution row stopped treating a plain `${...}` as a command. Under `enabled_detection_categories={"cmd_injection"}` it therefore passed. The row now flags it again; a plain variable reference (`${a}`, `${HOME}`) stays unflagged, as in the current row.
- **One invalid prefix no longer discards a provider's whole range set (LEDGER 6.375).** The AWS, GCP and Azure fetchers parsed every prefix with the strict `ip_network()` inside one `try`, so a single prefix with host bits set emptied the provider (the cloud block then protected nothing until the next good fetch). The invalid prefix is now skipped and one WARNING names how many were skipped and the first; the valid ones are kept, as the Vultr, DigitalOcean and Linode loops already did.
- **`@usage_monitor`, `@return_monitor` and `@behavior_analysis` report a threshold or window below 1 (LEDGER 6.374).** They accepted `0` or a negative value silently, where `@rate_limit` and `@suspicious_frequency` report the same shape through the misconfiguration check. They now raise `ValueError` with `fail_on_misconfiguration=True` and log one WARNING with it off, naming the decorator.
- **A CORS override record pulled from Redis is checked like a `configure()` call (LEDGER 6.372).** Only the CSP, HSTS, custom and named-header values of a pulled record were checked: `"enable_cors": "false"` (a string) switched CORS on and `cors_max_age: -5` was served as `Access-Control-Max-Age: -5`. The pull now runs the same CORS value checks `configure()` runs, refuses a record that fails them with the existing `Ignoring an unreadable security headers override in Redis` warning and keeps the override that was applied.
- **A behavior throttle never blocks in passive mode, and its Redis-outage block answers like the pipeline (LEDGER 6.370, 6.371).** With `passive_mode=True`, `fail_secure=True` and a Redis outage the throttle answered 500 where the pipeline never blocks in passive mode. It now logs the error and admits the request in passive mode, and with `fail_secure=True` otherwise answers the error's own status (503 for a Redis outage) with `Retry-After: 5`, as the pipeline does, instead of a fixed 500 with no `Retry-After`. The log lines are unchanged.
- **500 base64 gzip bombs in one JSON body cost less CPU than in 4.4.0 (LEDGER 6.363, R1d-02).** A 224 KB body of 500 values, each the base64 of a gzip of 300 KB of zeros, cost 4.45 s of CPU (4.4.0: 2.36 s). The printable-character count of each decoded block ran one Python call per character; an all-ASCII block is now counted in C by deleting the non-printable bytes (the same count), and the same body costs 2.1 s, the 100-value body 0.44 s against 1.55 s in 4.4.0.
- **Bytes appended after a gzip or zlib stream stay in the scan (LEDGER 6.363, R1d-04).** The inflated body replaced the wire body, so a payload placed after the end of the compressed stream (`gzip.compress(...) + b"\x00<script>..."`) was dropped. The bytes after the end of the stream are now part of the scanned body.
- **A command between two binary blocks is flagged whatever white space pads it (LEDGER 6.363, R1d-03).** A raw body of 4 KB of high bytes, 8 or more spaces or newlines, `; cat /etc/passwd`, the same padding and 4 KB of high bytes passed, while the same body padded with `x` was flagged: the scan text has its white space runs collapsed, so the padded command sat one character from the binary and counted as noise inside it. A match with white space on both sides now has a text neighbourhood, as one padded with letters had.
- **A raw zlib request body is inflated and scanned like a raw gzip body (LEDGER 6.363, R1d-04).** Only a gzip stream (`1f 8b`) was inflated, so `zlib.compress("<script>...")` sent as `application/octet-stream` passed while the same bytes in base64 or as gzip were flagged. A body that starts with a valid zlib header (`78` and a valid check) is now inflated within the same cap, with the same cap outcome, before the scan; a body that merely starts with `x` is not touched.
- **The once-a-minute sampling of an `X-Forwarded-For` from an untrusted peer is keyed by identity, so an IPv6 client rotating inside its /64 is one peer (LEDGER 6.354, R3r2-7).** The sample was keyed by the full peer address, so a client that rotated its address inside its /64 was sampled (logged and reported) on every request, up to the 10,000-peer table cap. It is now keyed by the client identity (the `ipv6_prefix_length` prefix for IPv6, the address for IPv4).
- **The GeoIP database download lock outlives the download and a worker releases only its own (LEDGER 6.354, R3r2-8).** The Redis lock lasted 60 s while a download may run 300 s, so a second worker could download the 128 MB file again, and the first worker's release deleted the second worker's lock. The lock now lasts 360 s (longer than the 300 s download cap) and carries a token; the release deletes it only when it still holds this worker's token.
- **An unavailable GeoIP reader logs one WARNING a minute, without the client address (LEDGER 6.354, R3r2-6).** While the database is not loaded, every `get_country` call logged a WARNING with the client IP: attacker-set log volume and personal data in the log. The line is now sampled once a minute per manager and names "the requesting client" instead of the address; the text is otherwise unchanged.
- **`check_rate_limit_by_ip` auto-bans into the caller's Redis when no guarded app holds the config (LEDGER 6.354, R3r2-4).** The ban went to the process-wide ban manager, which has no Redis handler when no guarded app runs in the process, so the rate-limit counters were shared through Redis while the ban stayed in one process (no `banned_ips` key). The ban is now written through a manager bound to the Redis handler the caller passed.
- **Without Redis, an expired exact-IP ban no longer hides a live network ban (LEDGER 6.354, R3r2-5).** The lookup ended with "not banned" at the first expired exact entry, before the operator CIDR ban and the grouped IPv6 identity ban were consulted, so the first request after an exact ban expired passed and the second was banned. The expired entry is now purged and the lookup goes on to the network and grouped bans.
- **A plain `http://` `agent_endpoint` on a host that is not this machine logs a WARNING, and the `blacklist` description matches its behavior (LEDGER 6.350, R4-10, R4-12).** The agent API key and the payload signature travel to `agent_endpoint`; over `http://` to another host they go unencrypted, and a mistyped endpoint received them without a word. Construction with the agent on now logs one WARNING for that case (`localhost`, `127.0.0.1` and `::1` and every `https://` endpoint are silent; nothing is refused, so private-network setups keep working). The `blacklist` field description said an explicit whitelist match overrides it, while the `whitelist` description and the engine say the blacklist always denies; it now says so.
- **A static `blocked_countries` or `whitelist_countries` entry that is not a two-letter code is refused (LEDGER 6.350, R4-9).** The static lists upper-cased every entry and accepted `USA`, `U1`, `" us"`, `None` and an empty string, which never match a country, while a dynamic rule with the same entries was rejected whole. A static entry now has to be two ASCII letters (any case, upper-cased as before), at construction and on assignment.
- **A `rate_limit` or `rate_limit_window` below 1 is refused only while `enable_rate_limiting` is on, and the 5.0.0 note says what 4.4.0 did (LEDGER 6.350, R4-7).** The refusal also fired with the limiter off, where the value has no effect, and the note said the old behavior was one WARNING when 4.4.0 logged nothing for these inputs. The same limit applies to a `endpoint_rate_limits` entry; the note now describes both behaviors as they were.
- **An assignment to a `SecurityConfig` field and `model_copy(update=...)` meet the rules construction meets (LEDGER 6.350, R4-6).** Both skipped the `Field` constraints of every field outside the revalidated set, the cross-field rules and the 5.0.0 refusal of a misconfiguration, so `config.ipv6_prefix_length = 0`, `config.detection_max_scan_seconds = 0`, `config.rate_limit = 0`, `config.exclude_paths = ["/a/*"]` or `config.enable_dynamic_rules = True` without an agent were accepted where the constructor raises, and `config.ban_on_hostile_signals = "no"` kept the truthy string. An assignment or a copy now raises the same error as the constructor (with `fail_on_misconfiguration=False` a new misconfiguration logs one WARNING instead), the field keeps its value and the revision does not move when it raises, and a `bool` field reads an assigned string as construction does.
- **`repr(config)` and `str(config)` no longer print the secrets (LEDGER 6.350, R4-5).** `redis_url` (with the password in it), `ipinfo_token`, `agent_api_key`, `agent_project_encryption_key` and `agent_payload_signing_secret` were part of the `SecurityConfig` repr, so a `print(config)`, a debug log or a traceback with locals showed them. The five fields are now left out of the repr; their values and every other use are unchanged.
- **A CSP or HSTS setting that could add a directive or a header line is refused (LEDGER 6.350, R4-4).** The 5.0.0 refusal of header values a response cannot carry covered the named headers and the custom headers but not the policies built from `csp` and `hsts`: a source such as `'self'\r\nX-Evil: 1`, a source with `;`, a directive name with a space or a line break, or an `hsts` `max_age` such as `1\r\nX-Evil2: 2` was written into the response header as it was. `SecurityConfig(security_headers=...)` and `configure()` now refuse a directive name that is not a token, a source that is not a string or carries `;` or a control character, and an `hsts` `max_age` that is not a non-negative integer.
- **A dynamic rule with an `ip_ban_duration` or `auto_ban_duration` below 1 is rejected as a whole (LEDGER 6.350, R4-3).** The rule passed the malformed-value gate and was reported as applied, and then every ban it asked for failed with `ban duration must be positive`, logged only. The gate now lists the duration like the other malformed values, so the rule is refused and the previous one stays.
- **A route-config container edit moves the revision once the change is in (LEDGER 6.346, R2-10).** The revision was bumped before a decorator container (a list, dict or set of a route) changed, so on the sync tree a request thread could see the new revision, rebuild its checks from the old contents and store that revision, and the change then landed with no further bump: a check whose `applies_to` depends on the container was never added. Every tracked mutation now bumps after the change (once, also when the mutation raises), so a reader that sees the new revision sees the new contents and one that read the contents in between rebuilds again.
- **The `endpoint` tag of the request metrics is bounded (LEDGER 6.346, R2-9).** The tag was the redacted raw request path, so a scanner requesting many distinct paths created one metric series per path in the agent and in the OTEL and Logfire exports. A request that matched a route now reports its route template (`/users/{id}`), and the paths no route matched share one `other` series after the first 1000 distinct ones; below that cap the value is the redacted path as before.
- **The ecosystem gate's expected-failures judge matches a listed message at the start of the failure text, and a bare assert exactly (LEDGER 6.346, R2-8).** An entry accepted any failure of the listed test whose text contained its `message` anywhere, so the bare `assert False is True` of a time-window test also accepted a different regression of that test. A message now has to start the failure text, and an entry with `"exact": true` has to equal it; the bare-assert entries of the three expected files are exact.
- **Emergency mode holds on `@bypass(["all"])` routes (LEDGER 6.346, R2-7).** A route with `@bypass(["all"])` is answered before the check pipeline runs, so `emergency_mode=True` did not lock it down while the documentation says emergency mode blocks all requests. The bypass path now takes the same verdict as the pipeline's emergency check (503 with `Retry-After: 60` for a client no whitelist admits, the same log line, `emergency_mode_block` event and `on_block` call; passive mode only logs) before it calls the route. With emergency mode off nothing changes. `@bypass(["all"])` still skips the ban list, the IP lists, rate limiting and detection, as its name says (use the narrower tokens `ip_ban`, `ip`, `clouds`, `rate_limit`, `penetration` to keep any of them).
- **Logfire metrics carry the request path as `endpoint` (LEDGER 6.346, R2-6).** The Logfire handler read `endpoint` from the metric's own field, which is `None` (the path is in `tags["endpoint"]`), and then dropped the tag, so every metric it forwarded had `endpoint=None`. The tag now supplies the value, as it already did in the OTEL handler.
- **A CR or LF in a flagged value or a log reason no longer forges a log line (LEDGER 6.346, R2-5).** The hit log wrote the first 100 characters of the flagged value (and any `reason`) with its line breaks intact, so a query parameter such as `<script>...\n2026-10-30 12:00:00 CRITICAL forged admin login ok` started a second, attacker-written line in a text log. Every line the engine writes through its level helper now shows CR as `\r` and LF as `\n`; a message without a line break is unchanged.
- **A `Content-Length` of more than 4300 digits answers 400 Invalid Content-Length instead of a 500 (LEDGER 6.346, R2-4).** `parse_declared_length` accepted the digits and then `int()` raised past Python's integer-string limit, so the 400 path was skipped and the request got the `fail_secure` 500 and an error log line. A value past the limit is now not a usable length, like a non-numeric one.
- **A custom validator that returns a truthy value that is not a response blocks the request with a 403 (LEDGER 6.345, behavior change).** The check logged `Custom validation failed` and sent a `decorator_violation` event with `action_taken="request_blocked"` for a validator that returned a dict, `True`, a string or a response object that does not satisfy `GuardResponse`, and then let the request through, so the log and the event claimed a block that did not happen. The request is now answered `403 Custom validation failed`, as the log and the event say; passive mode still only logs. A validator that returns a `GuardResponse` or `None` behaves as before.
- **A check added with `pipeline.add_check` or `insert_check`, and a check removed with `remove_check`, survives a config or route-config revision (LEDGER 6.344).** The pipeline rebuilt its check list from the config on every revision and replaced the whole list, so a check a caller had added by hand was dropped at the next change (`cfg.exclude_paths = [...]`, a route-config edit) and a removed check came back. The edits are now recorded and replayed, in order, on each rebuilt list.
- **A security-headers override record in Redis with a value the headers builder cannot read is refused when it is pulled (R4-r2, traced).** A record such as `{"csp": "..."}` (a string, not a directive map), a `hsts` that is not an object, a `custom` that is not a header map or a header setting that is not a string was kept and then raised from the headers builder on every response build until the record was replaced. The pull now reads the record through the same checks, logs one `Ignoring an unreadable security headers override in Redis` warning and keeps the override that was applied.
- **A `return_pattern` rule with a `regex:` pattern goes through the ReDoS validator at construction, and a rule that could never match is refused (LEDGER 6.347).** A behavior rule such as `regex:(a+)+$` ran `re.search` on the event loop over up to the whole inspected response body (a 27-byte reflected body held the loop for 4.5 s of CPU), and `regex:(` or `status:4xx` was accepted and then logged an ERROR for every evaluated response without ever matching. `SecurityConfig(global_behavior_rules=...)`, an assignment to it, `return_monitor` and `behavior_analysis` now refuse a `regex:` pattern the validator rejects or that does not compile, and a `status:` pattern that is not an integer, with a message that names the rule. Safe patterns and every `json:`, plain-text and `status:<int>` rule are unchanged.
- **An IPv4-mapped network in a list applies to IPv4 clients, and a mapped or NAT64 loopback is refused as an exempt entry (LEDGER 6.348).** A `::ffff:1.2.3.0/120`, `::ffff:1.2.3.4/128` or `::ffff:1.0.0.0/104` entry in `blacklist`, `whitelist`, `trusted_proxies` or a route list never matched a client that reaches the engine as plain IPv4, so a blacklist entry written that way let `1.2.3.4` through (fail-open) and a whitelist entry never admitted it. Every mapped network of prefix length 96 or more is now stored and matched as the IPv4 network it stands for (`::ffff:1.2.3.0/120` becomes `1.2.3.0/24`). The dynamic-rule exempt guard that refuses loopback now also refuses `::ffff:127.0.0.1` and `64:ff9b::7f00:1` (and the networks that contain them), as it refused `127.0.0.1`. A client address that is itself written in the mapped form (`::ffff:1.2.3.4`) is matched against the IPv4 entries of a list too.
- **5.0.0: the default `X-XSS-Protection` is `0`, it was `1; mode=block` (LEDGER 6.349, release note, behavior).** Browsers have removed their XSS auditors and the legacy `1; mode=block` filter has been used to attack pages, so a response without your own value now carries `X-XSS-Protection: 0`. To keep the 4.x header, set `security_headers={"enabled": True, "xss_protection": "1; mode=block"}` (checked with `apply_security_headers_settings`: the response header is `1; mode=block`).
- **A Redis URL's `?password=` and `?username=` no longer reach event metadata or warnings (LEDGER 6.352).** `_redact_redis_url` stripped the userinfo but kept the query string, where redis-py also reads `password` and `username`, so a URL written that way leaked its credentials into the `redis_connection` and `redis_error` events and the shared-prefix warnings. Those two parameters (any case) are now dropped; every other parameter is kept as written.
- **A ban kept in memory because its Redis write failed is no longer lost when another worker moves the ban version (LEDGER 6.353).** A worker that saw the shared ban version move cleared its whole local ban cache and reloaded from Redis, which dropped a ban that only this process held (the write had failed) and let that client through for the rest of the ban. The reload now drops only the bans Redis also holds and keeps the memory-only and foreign-memory ones; a lookup that finds one still answers banned.
- **A failed pending-ban read no longer skips the checks after it (LEDGER 6.343).** When the ban read the IP check had left pending failed with `redis_fail_open=True`, `fail_secure=False` or passive mode, the error was handled as that next check's own error: the check was skipped (it could be penetration detection) and, because the read stayed pending, every later check up to the next one that defers the ban read was skipped too, and the final read hit the same error. The resolve now has its own error handling: the failure is logged and answered exactly as before, then the next check runs. Under `fail_secure=True` the request is still answered 503.
- **The UTF-7 decoder is linear on a long run of `+` (LEDGER 6.362).** The shifted-run decoder retried its pattern from every `+` of a run that has no closing `-`, so 80 KB of `+` took 7.8 s of CPU, 160 KB took 30.5 s and a 40 KB header or query value 1.9 s in one stretch that `detection_max_scan_seconds` cannot interrupt (v4.4.0: 0.23 s at 160 KB). It now reads each base64 run once and decodes the same runs with the same output, so 160 KB of `+` costs well under 0.5 s.
- **The Redis pipeline rate-limit fallback counts a concurrent burst exactly (LEDGER 6.351).** When the rate-limit Lua script is not loaded (a failed `SCRIPT LOAD` at startup) and for `check_rate_limit_by_ip`, the fallback read the window in one round trip and recorded the hit in a second, so concurrent requests all saw the same count and let more than the limit through (limit 5, 8 in flight: 9 of 40 and 16 of 200 allowed; v4.4.0 allowed 5). It now records the hit and counts the window in one transactional pipeline, as v4.4.0 did, and withdraws the hit again when the request is over the limit, so a blocked request is still not recorded. The verdict, the request count and the `Retry-After` value are unchanged.
- **A forked worker no longer inherits its parent's regex worker pool, and a Redis client built in one event loop is rebuilt for the next (LEDGER 6.405, 6.406).** A worker forked after its parent had already scanned a request kept the parent's pool counts and locks but none of its threads, so the child's first scanned request was queued behind workers that do not exist and answered 400; a cold fork was clean. The detection compiler now forgets the parent's workers in the child (`os.register_at_fork`), so the first scan builds its own. With Redis on, a handler's client was kept for the loop that built it, so a second `asyncio.run` or a warm fork with a new loop answered its first request 503 before it recovered. The Redis handler now records the loop its client was built in and rebuilds the client when the running loop is another one; a client with no recorded loop is left alone.
- **A Redis client left behind by a finished event loop no longer leaks its sockets (LEDGER 6.441).** The rebuild for a new loop dropped the old client without closing it, so its connections stayed open until garbage collection and every `-W error` run of an adapter that ran an app in a second loop failed with `ResourceWarning: unclosed <socket.socket>`. The handler now releases the old client's sockets: on its own loop's thread when that loop is still running, and from the new loop when the old one is stopped or closed. It never blocks the new loop, and the first request in the new loop is still served.
- **The loop that judges a row's candidates ends the scan when `detection_max_scan_seconds` is spent (WP302).** WP298 made that loop charge the scan budget and yield, but it kept judging candidates after the budget was gone and a spent budget on the last row passed as clean. It now ends the scan with the same `scan_time_budget_exhausted` outcome the pattern loop reports (and the fallback check reports as budget exhausted). The detection handler's regex module also moved its row weights, candidate gates and windowed finders into sibling modules (`_suspatterns_weights`, `_suspatterns_candidate_gates`, `_suspatterns_windowed_finders`), every name still importable from `_suspatterns_regex`, so every module ranks A for maintainability on Python 3.10 to 3.15.
- **A body of repeated `$(a)`, `${a}` or `<?php`, each followed by a space, no longer stalls the event loop (WP298).** The `$(...)`/`${...}` row and the glob wildcard row cost time proportional to the match's position in the value for every match, so a 40 KB text body of `$(a)` and a space, repeated, took 12.7 s of CPU in one uninterrupted stretch (v4.4.0: 13.2 s) and a 240 KB body of `<?php` and a space, repeated, took 16.3 s, longer than `detection_max_scan_seconds`. Each match now looks only at the few characters around it, so the 40 KB body costs 0.05 s and the 240 KB body 0.2 s, and the loop that judges a row's candidates charges the scan budget and yields to the event loop once a slice has elapsed, like the pattern loop. Every verdict is unchanged: the validators return the same answer on 20000 generated strings and on every string of the test and fixture corpora, and the engine's threats are equal on 16000 string and context pairs.
- **Attack-region extraction counts against `detection_max_scan_seconds`, and the body preprocessing yields the event loop between its steps (LEDGER 6.285, 6.287).** The CPU cap documented as summed across every field and pattern did not include the passes that find the attack regions of a body larger than the full-scan window, so a clean 20 MB JSON body on a route with a raised `max_request_size` spent 12.2 s of CPU with no budget outcome. That CPU is now charged to the request's budget, and once the budget is spent the request gets the same `scan_time_budget_exhausted` `pattern_timeout` the regex passes already give. The preprocessing of a large value (the decode steps, the whitespace pass, the attack-region passes, the extra views) also yields to the event loop once the 2 ms slice has passed, with identical output: the largest event-loop stall of a 4 MB clean body fell from 0.72-0.96 s to 0.23 s. The base64 candidate pass is still one uninterrupted call, because plain text with spaces is one candidate token for it, so the stall has that floor.
- **The log and event preview of a large value is redacted from its first 4096 characters, and the whitespace, overlong-percent and base64 passes yield the event loop (LEDGER 6.289, 6.287).** A threat redacted the whole body to show 100 characters, 6.6 s of CPU and a 7 s loop stall on a 20 MB body; the preview now redacts the first 4096 characters (a secret running past that edge, including an unclosed XML element, is still redacted), and text up to 4096 characters is unchanged. The three preprocessing passes now yield between windows with identical output.
- **An app's events carry only the dynamic rule set that app applied (LEDGER 6.268).** The view of the applied rule set that stamps `matched_rule_type` and `matched_rule_id` on events was one process-wide global, so with two apps in a process a block in app B carried the rule id of the set app A had applied. The view now lives on the app's own `DynamicRuleManager`, found from the request's config the way the per-app managers are, and `set_attribution_view(view, config=None)` still sets the default manager's view when no config is given.
- **Each app reports its own sampled warnings and events (LEDGER 6.269).** The once-per-window tables for the untrusted `X-Forwarded-For` warning and event, the detection-disabled event, the emergency-mode lines, the Redis failure during initialization, the failed ban attribution line and the refused response body report were process-wide, so app B stayed silent about a peer or reason app A had already reported in the window. Each table is now held by its app's config (a copy of the config shares it), with the same window, rate, caps per app and log text; a process with N apps holds at most N tables of each kind.
- **Two apps in one process each log their own first warning (LEDGER 6.284).** The log-once warning about a stored ban that is not enforced for an app and the 5.0.0 changed-defaults warning kept their "already logged" sets process-wide, so the first app to hit a condition hid the same warning for every other app. Each app now keeps its own sets, found by its config, and a copy of the config shares them; the log lines are unchanged. The pulled security-headers override check that moves an app onto the slow path now asks whether this app has a pulled record, not whether any app has one, so a pull by one app no longer slows the others (their headers are the same either way).
- **Each app decodes its own clients with its own `nat64_prefixes` (LEDGER 6.294).** The table of network-specific NAT64 prefixes was one process-wide global that every app replaced at startup, so with two apps in one process the app that started last decided the embedded-IPv4 identity (lists, bans, rate limits) of every request, and a first app with `nat64_prefixes` set went back to seeing its translated clients as IPv6 addresses once a second app started. Each app now keeps its own table, found by its config (a copy of the config shares it), the cached decodings are keyed on it, and the once-a-minute warning about an embedded non-global IPv4 address is kept per app. The well-known prefix `64:ff9b::/96` is still decoded for every app and the log line is unchanged.
- **`check_rate_limit_by_ip` keeps its window and strikes per app (LEDGER 6.278).** The in-memory request windows and the rate-limit strikes of the request-free primitive were two process-wide dicts keyed by IP only, so with two apps in a process the first call of app B from an IP was refused on app A's budget and app B's auto-ban counter took app A's violations. Each app now holds its own windows and strikes, found from the config the call is made with, and a copy of the config shares them. The Redis keys are unchanged. Clearing, unbanning or forgetting strikes still reaches every app's tables.
- **A U+FFFD standing where an attack's lead byte was no longer hides a pickle `GLOBAL` payload or an overlong-encoded traversal (LEDGER 6.291).** A framework decodes a raw high byte it cannot read to U+FFFD, so a body or query string carrying a pickle opcode byte, or `/..%c0%2fconfig.yaml` and `/..%c0%afconfig.yaml` percent-decoded by the framework with replacement, reached the engine as text with a U+FFFD where the attack byte had been and returned no verdict (the same at v4.4.0), while the surrogate-escaped form was flagged. A U+FFFD in the prefix before a pickle `GLOBAL` row now stands for any opcode byte `0x80` to `0x95` or a `0xFF` filler, and the prefix passes when some choice walks as a pickle opcode stream (at most 8 U+FFFD and 256 walks, then no flag; `guard_core/handlers/_suspatterns_pickle.py:125-190`). The decoded traversal views read a `..` that opens a path segment followed by one or two lost bytes and a separator, or two lost bytes and a word character, as `../` (`repair_lost_overlong_bytes`, `guard_core/detection_engine/encoding_decoders.py:158-190`). Both run only when the content holds a U+FFFD (`guard_core/handlers/suspatterns_handler.py:986`, `guard_core/handlers/_suspatterns_views.py:105`), so clean requests cost no extra call. The cost is that a plain relative path whose `..` segment is followed by a U+FFFD (`../` with the slash lost) is flagged, because that is the shape of the attack. A U+FFFD in the part of a pickle stream after the `GLOBAL` row is still not read.
- **`unban_ip` no longer raises on a string that is not a valid network (LEDGER 6.295).** `unban_ip` sends a value that contains `/` to the network path only when `ipaddress.ip_network` accepts it (`_network_of_cidr_text`, `guard_core/handlers/_ipban_queries.py:46-52`, used at `:287`); `/`, `web/xml` and `1.2.3.4/33` take the exact-address path, change no ban state and send the unban event with the text given, as in v4.4.0 (the network path added in 5.0.0 raised `ValueError` for these values before this fix, so an admin route that passed request input to `unban_ip` answered 500). A valid network such as `10.0.0.0/8` still lifts that network ban and clears no strikes.
- **A second app's ban manager no longer becomes the class singleton (LEDGER 6.234, 5.51).** `initialize_redis` set `IPBanManager._instance` to the manager that ran it, so once an app on another Redis prefix initialized its own manager, `IPBanManager()` returned that manager and an unbound config (one no app was bound to) auto-banned into the other app's store and Redis prefix instead of the default manager's. The class singleton stays the default manager (`ip_ban_manager`) and a per-app manager never rebinds it.
- **`agent_stats` answers with the agent on (LEDGER 6.232).** Once the engine swapped in its `CompositeAgentHandler`, the adapters' `agent_stats` raised `AttributeError` because the composite had no `get_stats`. The composite now returns the stats of the handlers that have one (the agent's, unchanged), `{}` when none does, and `AgentHandlerProtocol` declares `get_stats() -> dict[str, JsonValue]` so adapters call it without a cast.
- **Two apps on one `redis_prefix` keep their own events, Redis writes and config (LEDGER 6.209).** Before this fix, pattern events carrying client IPs from app A could reach app B's agent and its SaaS project: apps on the same prefix share their managers, and each app's init rebound the shared manager's agent, Redis handler and config, so the last app initialized served every app. The agent, Redis handler and config of the app now travel in one per-request scope that the pipeline sets where the checks start, the shared managers read them from it, and an app's init runs in its own scope, and each app now has its own dynamic-rule manager and loop, so a dynamic-rule ban from app B goes through B's handlers. A shared manager called with no app in scope answers no agent or Redis handler instead of the last app's: a manual `ban_ip()`, `unban_ip()` or `is_ip_banned()` there is resolved across every sharing app (refused when any sharing app refuses, written and removed under every identity they resolve) and logs one warning, and all three accept `config=` to run as one app. The dynamic-rule `ip_whitelist` exemption is kept per app, so one app's rule no longer exempts another app's bans. A ban another app or process recorded on an address this app trusts as a proxy (or on loopback) is not enforced for this app, so a health check from its own proxy is no longer blocked; every app that does not trust the address still enforces it, and one warning per address names it and the prefix. Apps share a manager only when their Redis connection settings match (the key the connection pool uses, event loop included); the same prefix on different connections gets separate managers and one startup warning naming the prefix and both URLs with credentials removed.
- **A wrong-typed config value now raises a `ValidationError` at its field instead of a raw `TypeError` (LEDGER 6.205).** `SecurityConfig(log_sensitive_params=42)` raised `TypeError: 'int' object is not iterable`, because the validators called `frozenset(v)`, `dict(v)`, `ThreatBanConfig(**value)` or iterated the entries of `whitelist`, `blacklist`, `exempt_ips` and `trusted_proxies` on any input, and pydantic turns only a `ValueError` into a `ValidationError`, so a caller catching `ValidationError` for bad config got an unhandled `TypeError` (13 fields in the probe, the same in v4.4.0). `enabled_detection_categories`, `muted_event_types`, `muted_metric_types`, `muted_check_logs`, `log_sensitive_headers`, `log_sensitive_params`, `log_sensitive_body_fields`, `block_cloud_providers`, `threat_ban_config` and the four IP lists now raise a `ValueError` naming the field, and an unknown-name message with mixed-type entries no longer fails while sorting them. Valid input and every existing error message are unchanged.
- **Two apps in one process keep the cloud provider detail and the sensitive names of the pattern redaction apart.** `check_ip_access` read the blocked-provider name and network from the module-level `cloud_handler` while the block decision came from the manager of the app's config, so the two could come from different managers; both now use `cloud_handler_for(config)`. A per-app `SusPatternsManager` now keeps its own `log_sensitive_*` name sets instead of adding them to the class, and the pattern text in a detection message is redacted with the names of the app being scanned, not the union of every app in the process. The module-level objects stay the default app's. The log lines a per-app manager writes outside a scan (dynamic rule planning and application, pattern add and remove, pattern timeouts, the pattern event) redact with that app's own names as well. So do the performance monitor's pattern reports, anomaly data and anomaly events, the ReDoS arbiter's log of a structural-rule disagreement, and the pattern text in a `ValueError` from a config's `blocked_user_agents` and `return_pattern` checks and from `block_user_agents`.
- **Two apps on their own `redis_prefix` keep custom patterns, dynamic-rule state, cloud-range and GeoIP caches apart.** `SusPatternsManager`, `DynamicRuleManager`, `CloudManager` and `IPInfoManager` were process singletons holding the config or Redis handler of one app, so app A's custom patterns and last-known dynamic rules landed under app B's prefix, and its cloud-range cache under the first app's. `sus_patterns_manager_for(config)` and `cloud_handler_for(config)` resolve the manager of an app the way `ip_ban_manager_for(config)` does: the module-level `sus_patterns_handler` and `cloud_handler` stay the default ones, and an app that uses Redis under another prefix, or no Redis, gets a manager of its own, found by config identity (Redis state follows the prefix, settings follow the config object). A no-Redis app never moves the prefix the default serves, and the request path reads its pattern manager from its config without a lookup, so two apps no longer rebuild the detection state on every alternating request. `DynamicRuleManager(config)` does the same, and one `IPInfoManager` serves each database path (the first app's token and the smallest `max_age`). The compiled built-in patterns, the regex thread pool, the provider ranges (downloaded once for N apps) and the GeoIP reader (opened once) stay shared. A GeoIP database shared by several apps is now cached under every app's prefix, not only the last one's, and a per-app manager is held for as long as its app's Redis handler, so the middle app of three no longer falls back to the first app's managers (this also closes that gap for the per-app ban manager). Apps on one Redis prefix share the default as before.
- **Two apps on their own `redis_prefix` no longer share bans.** `IPBanManager` was one process singleton bound to the config and Redis handler of the last app, so a ban written through app A could land under app B's prefix. `ip_ban_manager_for(config)` now resolves the ban manager of the app: the module-level `ip_ban_manager` stays the default one, and an app that uses Redis under a different prefix than the app the default serves gets a manager of its own, found by config identity. The checks, the rate-limit auto-ban, the behavior ban action, the dynamic rule handler and the initializer resolve it per config. `IPBanManager._instance` now means the most recently initialized manager, and `reset_global_state()` forgets the default binding. The cloud, geo, pattern and dynamic-rule handlers are still one per process.
- **Two apps in one process keep Redis state under their own `redis_prefix`.** `RedisManager` was one process singleton that took the config of the last construction, so app A's bans, rate limits and strikes landed under app B's prefix. It is now one manager per `SecurityConfig` object; managers with equal connection settings on one event loop share one client and pool, so N apps on one Redis keep today's single pool. `RedisManager._instance` stays and means the most recently initialized manager. fastapi-guard 8.1.2's websocket path resolves its handler from `_instance` (`guard/websocket.py:303` to `guard/lifespan.py:48-56`) and so sees the last initialized app's handler; fastapi-guard's next major resolves the handler from its own middleware.
- **Country rules work on the sync engine and on routes without a global rule** (LEDGER 6.157, 6.158). The sync engine (flaskapi-guard, djapi-guard) never awaited the async `IPInfoManager` that `SecurityConfig` builds from `ipinfo_token`, so the database never loaded and `blocked_countries` never blocked. The engine now swaps that handler for `guard_core.sync.handlers.ipinfo_handler.IPInfoManager` (same token, `ipinfo_db_path` and `geo_ip_db_max_age`) when its `HandlerInitializer` is built, and a user-supplied async handler reaching the sync engine raises a `ValueError` at startup that names the sync class to use. `geo_ip_handler` also accepts the sync handler type, so `geo_ip_handler=IPInfoManager(token)` with the sync class type-checks without a cast. A route-level `block_countries`, `allow_countries` or `geo_rate_limit` with `ipinfo_token` set and no global country rule now gets a handler built from the token when the decorators are read at startup (no database download when no rule uses it), and adapters keep `config.geo_ip_handler` whenever one is configured. A route that carries a country rule or a geo rate limit loads the GeoIP database on demand before it reads a country, exactly as the global rules do, so a route-only `block_countries` blocks and a route-only `allow_countries` admits with the default `lazy_init=True` and no Redis; routes without those rules run no new code.
- **A route or geo limit no longer double counts** when both are set on one route (each tier has its own bucket), `Retry-After` is the time until the blocking tier has room again instead of the full window, and `geo_rate_limit` country keys are uppercased and resolved with the same geo handler as the IP checks.
